We shall speak of the following matters when we treat the properties of the signs: what arises from these images in each of the <term>decans</term><note>A decan is a subdivision of a zodiac sign, covering ten degrees.</note> of the signs. From this, it is found through the <term>astrolabe</term><note>A historical astronomical instrument used for measuring the altitudes of celestial bodies and solving problems of time and position.</note> which of their stars occupy which degrees of the signs, while their natures and effects are to be treated elsewhere.

### ¶ Why the 12 signs in the zodiac hold leadership over the others. Chapter 2.

<image-desc>Decorative woodcut initial 'H' featuring a central human face surrounded by stylized floral scrollwork.</image-desc>
Having arranged these matters in this way, it follows that we should explain by what reasoning, out of the whole heaven, the twelve signs hold leadership over all things above the rest. For although, as has been said, forty-eight images <note>The 48 Ptolemaic constellations.</note> complete the entire heaven, nevertheless these twelve, which surround the middle of the heaven and the earth, have by right surpassed the others in power, having drawn the forces of the others to themselves. For there are those who doubt the reason why, among all others, these twelve have been granted power as if the others were doing nothing. To them, I believe I can respond sufficiently that no author of this art, or any other, either thinks or asserts that any part of the heaven is entirely immune from duty in the world. Rather, these twelve hold the universal and common leadership of things, while the others hold leadership in certain singular and private properties of things, which is established by several diverse reasons.

¶ In the first place, because the <term>zodiac circle</term><note>original: "circulus signifer", literally the 'sign-bearing circle'.</note> surrounding the world encircles its center—that is, the globe of the earth in the middle—with a daily revolution. We see that the generations and corruptions of things follow the indicators of this circuit. Since the other images are removed to either side of this world <note>i.e., north or south of the ecliptic</note>, the twelve signs rightly seem to hold sway. The others are not entirely without influence, according to the manner of their own circuit, but only in singular matters; for the universal care of things has been granted to the twelve. For a thing is "universal" when it pertains to the common accidents of genera and species, but "singular" when it pertains to the properties of individuals.

¶ In the second place, because the rising and setting of the Sun <note>original: "☉"</note> are followed by the accidents of things. The Sun, however, journeys only through these twelve; by the same writing, the leadership of the Sun is commended to the forces of these parts of the heaven above the others.

¶ In the third place, while the Sun travels through this circle, the circle of the year is completed. Through the seasons of the year, the generations and corruptions of things and other motions continue perpetually, so that through each individual sign, the steps or positions of the Sun alternate the causes of the world's accidents.

¶ In the fourth place, because the Moon <note>original: "☽"</note> and the other wandering stars <note>The planets.</note>, following the same path as the Sun and not diverting except for their latitudes, likewise add significantly to the alterations of the seasons and the accidents of things through each sign.

¶ In the fifth place, the leadership of none of the other images appears unless it is proportional to its own sign, to which it is in a way an appendage.

For these causes and reasons, since this circle has principally obtained the general leadership of the whole heaven, it was first divided into twelve equal intervals which we call "signs" (the others not being so divided), and then into 360 degrees divided into equal parts. Other divisions of parts necessary to the art follow. Indeed, a sign consists of 30 degrees; a degree of 60 minutes <note>original: "punctorum", literally 'points'.</note>; a minute of 60 seconds; a second of 60 thirds; and so on through fourths, fifths, sixths, up to tenths and twelfths or even further, where the quantities grow infinitely, to the detriment of one and the increase of another. For among numbers, 12, 30, 60, and 360 most easily admit any divisions, such as a third...

It also introduces the mathematical subdivisions of the zodiac into degrees, minutes, and seconds.</summary>

### Why these figures are twelve in number. #### Chapter 3.

Now it must be explained why neither more than twelve are necessary, nor fewer are seen to suffice for the guidance of things. To this, first, the authority of Aratus <note>Aratus of Soli (c. 315–240 BCE), a Greek didactic poet famous for his work 'Phaenomena' on astronomy.</note> is added. Who, when he described 48 celestial figures, distinguished among them twelve signs as the signs of the sun. ¶ Furthermore, as is read in philosophy, since whatever is born and dies in this world consists of a composition of the four elements <note>The classical elements: Fire, Earth, Air, and Water.</note>, which are led through intervals of a beginning, a middle, and an end; these three, multiplied by those four, produce twelve, by which twelve those signs provide guidance to the numbers of the signs. Since, therefore, it was fitting for the number of signs to correspond to that number, it was fitting that there be twelve. Indeed, these signs preside over the four elements and their three intervals; for the signs are the places of the stars, not having their own substance separately moving the generations and corruptions of things: but by rising and setting over the lower world and by receiving the wandering stars <note>The planets.</note>: thus also they do not dissolve the elements by themselves, nor do they produce the generations or corruptions of things, but the alterations of the seasons, the resolutions of the elements, and the causes of generations follow as a consequence. Thus, therefore, it was necessary for the signs of the world's circle to agree in fact and number with the four elements and their three intervals, which will be more clearly established by explaining: for Aries presides over fire; Taurus over earth; Gemini over air; Cancer over water; then, the beginning being repeated, Leo likewise over fire; Virgo over earth; Libra over air; Scorpio over water. And again, the order being repeated: Sagittarius over fire; Capricorn over earth; Aquarius over air; Pisces over water. Therefore, the first order of signs obtains the first interval of the elements, that is, the beginning of generation. The second order the second, that is, the middle of life. The third order the third <note>The end or corruption.</note>. There are, therefore, among the signs three indeed fiery, three earthy, three airy, and three aquatic. For Aries presides over the hot, dry, and vegetative, fit for life and for the increase and nourishment of living things and seeds; Leo over the less temperate, ripening the state of things. Sagittarius over the harmful, corrupting, and dissolving the framework of living things and seeds. Taurus over the cold and dry, a friend to generation like a fertile field and the nourishment of seeds and living things. Virgo over the useless and unfit, such as sterile fields and that sort. Capricorn over the harmful and dissolutive, such as refuse and heavy earth. Gemini over the warm, moist, and temperate, having attained its own generation of things and forms, such as sweet and comforting odors. Libra over the thick and turbulent, such as winds and fatty vapors. Aquarius over the heavy and corrupt, such as foul vapors and heavy air. Cancer over the moist, cold, temperate, and nonetheless sweet, suitable for the nature and nourishment of things, such as sustaining humors. Scorpio over the less capable, such as salty humors useless to the nature of living things and seeds. Pisces over the corrupt, distempered, and dissolved, such as marshes and filthy pools. Therefore, four preside over the generations of things, four over the middle between generation and corruption, and four over corruption; and thus each of the twelve has its own powers and properties in the various accidents of things from the three intervals of the four elements.

<summary>This chapter explains why there are exactly twelve zodiac signs, attributing the number to the four elements multiplied by the three stages of existence (beginning, middle, and end). It categorizes each sign by its elemental quality and its biological or environmental effect.</summary>

<keywords>Zodiac, Aratus, Four Elements, Astrology, Generation and Corruption, Aries, Taurus, Gemini, Cancer, Leo, Virgo, Libra, Scorpio, Sagittarius, Capricorn, Aquarius, Pisces</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of astrological and natural philosophy, specifically focusing on the elemental qualities of the zodiac signs.</meta>

# ¶ On the composition of these images. Chapter 4.

**Next**, it is appropriate to treat the nature of the signs. Indeed, some who devote themselves to natural science <note>naturali scientie: the study of the physical world and its laws</note> wonder about these things. Why, among the signs, is the natural order interrupted so that after a fiery sign, an airy one does not immediately follow, but rather an earthy one? To them, a reason of this kind responds not inaptly: that the simple elements—heat, cold, dryness, and moisture—are not themselves bodies. Rather, as they are the origin of all bodies, so are they the origin of the substantial composition of those things commonly called elements: earth, water, air, and fire. Although each of these is composed of several of those qualities, one nevertheless predominates in each. Just as in any fire, heat and dryness are present, yet heat predominates; so in earth, which is cold and dry, dryness predominates; so in water, cold; and in air, moisture is the weightier quality. Therefore, of the elements, heat and cold are active, while dryness and moisture are passive. 

As for the active qualities, they are contraries. Heat indeed drives the generation of things; cold drives their corruption <note>corruptionem: in this context, the breaking down or decay of matter</note>. As for the passive qualities, they are likewise opposites, yet dryness is more receptive to action. Since, therefore, the power and manner of the elements are of this kind, it is established by all reasons why the beginning is taken from fire in the composition of the signs. 

In the first place, because heat predominates in fire; and since neither the generation nor the movement of living beings exists except from heat, it rightfully obtained the first of the extremities. The other extremity is water, in which cold prevails. For just as heat administers generation and animal life, so cold—its contrary—is the destroyer of life and the corruption of offspring. 

In the second place: because heat is the element of generation, while cold is the element of corruption, and since generation is first in things and corruption is last, fire deservedly takes the first extremity and water the second. 

Thirdly, because all the passions <note>passiones: experiences or undergoings</note> of animal life are between the beginning and the end; the active qualities, being the beginning and the end, ought to hold the extremities, while the passive qualities hold the middle place. 

Fourthly, since the celestial bodies are the cause of all generation in this world, and among those bodies in the order of elements fire is first, the element of heat had to be first; while cold, which was its contrary, had to be last. Since, therefore, the active qualities of heat and cold are the extremes, it was necessary for the passive qualities—namely dryness and moisture—to be left in the middle. 

Because, truly, as heat abounds in fire, so dryness abounds in earth. It is necessary for an earthy sign to succeed a fiery sign immediately for two reasons. The first is the kinship of the elements. For dryness is joined to heat by a certain kinship. The second is from the primacy of each: for just as heat excels among the active qualities, so dryness prevails by a certain power among the passive. Since, therefore, in the order of the signs, fire possesses the first place and water the fourth, and earth succeeds fire in the second place, it is necessary for the third to be left to air. For these reasons, therefore, in the composition of the signs, the first is fiery, the second earthy, the third airy, and the fourth aquatic. Whence it is that we say Aries is hot and dry; Taurus is cold and dry; Gemini is hot and moist; Cancer is cold and moist; and so on through the order in this manner.

***

# ¶ Why they begin from Aries. Chapter 5.

**Then**, it must be introduced why the order of the signs begins from Aries, in which part a response is given to those who—because a circle has neither beginning nor end—think it should no more begin from Aries than from anywhere else. First, therefore, we shall explain the necessity...

<summary>This page explains the elemental logic behind the sequence of the zodiac signs, arguing that they follow a pattern of active and passive qualities (heat, cold, dryness, moisture) rather than a simple rotation of the four elements. It establishes fire (Aries) as the starting point of the zodiac due to its generative heat.</summary>
<keywords>zodiac, four elements, Aristotelian qualities, Aries, Taurus, Gemini, Cancer, generation and corruption, astrology, natural philosophy</keywords>

--- Page 33 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of celestial mechanics and the division of the zodiac, specifically focusing on why the solar year must begin at a fixed point to govern the cycles of life and death on Earth.</meta>

...that there was some beginning to the circle, and specifically that one point above all others. For since the simple elements are the principles of all bodies, being composed partly of generation <note>the process of coming into being</note> and partly of corruption <note>decay or dissolution</note>, they act accordingly. We measure the different spans of generation and corruption by the different seasons of the year. Therefore, to measure the continuation of time which follows the path of the sun, it was necessary to begin from a certain fixed place in the circle. Moreover, it is more fitting to begin from no other point than that from which the beginning of time moves the elements of generation.

¶ For indeed, when either of the active elements is mixed with either of the passive ones, it produces either generation or corruption: for example, heat with moisture creates the framework of nature and vital motion—generation and growth. The same heat with dryness brings the dissolution of nature and the corruption and death of life. Likewise, cold with moisture is agreeable to nature, while with dryness it is hostile; all of these continue without fail through the legitimate succession of the seasons. For from the entry of the sun into the first point of **Aries** <note>original: "arietis"</note>, the weather is tempered by heat and moisture, making it fit for all procreation; beginning through the three following signs of **Aries, Taurus, and Gemini**, it continues until the increase of the day is finished. As the sun migrates to the beginning of **Cancer**, the temperament of the air changes into a fiery nature of heat and dryness; the increase of the day from the first quadrant begins to pay back the decrease in equal measure through the order of signs until the end of **Virgo**. When the equinox has passed and the sun enters **Libra**, a cold and dry season succeeds, leading to the increase of the night until the end of **Sagittarius**. From there, as the sun descends into **Capricorn**, a cold and moist season follows; with the increase of the day on one hand and the decrease of the night on the other, they are brought back to equality at the end of **Pisces**, celebrating the equinox.

Thus, having emerged through the three hundred and sixty degrees in 365 days and a quarter (minus a 360th part of a day, as it seemed to **Ptolemy** <note>original: "Ptholomeo"</note>), the sun completes what we call the solar year of twelve months, according to the 12 signs of the circle. For the year is divided into four parts according to its kinship with the circle. **Spring** is of an airy quality, hot and moist, producing offspring and providing nourishment. **Summer** is of a fiery nature, hot and dry, less fit for procreation and inclining the state of things toward decay. **Autumn** is cold and dry, hostile to procreation. **Winter** is watery, the nourishment for the parts of spring. Each season has three intervals: a beginning, a middle, and an end, drawn through each sign of the circle. Indeed, the beginning is taken from the signs in which the alteration of the season occurs. From the second signs, the middle; from the third, the end. Therefore, it was necessary for the circle to have a certain beginning, and that one above all others, which would be the head of time and the moments of generations.

### ¶ On the Tropic, Fixed, and Bipartite Signs. Chapter 6.

-><image-desc>A large decorative woodcut initial 'C' featuring a ram (Aries) reclining and looking backward within the curve of the letter, set against a background of dense horizontal hatching.</image-desc><-

**It follows** that we should distinguish the signs from which the intervals of the seasons are divided by certain names and properties. For since the four quadrants of the year correspond in order to the four quadrants of the circle, and just as the quadrants of the circle are numbered by three signs, so the quadrants of the year are numbered by three intervals; it seems necessary that each should correspond to the other. For the beginning of a season occurs when the sun enters the beginning of a quadrant. For this reason, that sign is called **tropic** <note>from the Greek 'tropos' meaning a turn; the points where the sun 'turns' back toward the equator</note>; while the following sign, by which the same season...

<summary>This page explains the astrological and elemental basis for the four seasons, linking the sun's journey through the zodiac signs to the cycles of heat, moisture, growth, and decay. It establishes Aries as the starting point of the solar year and introduces the classification of signs into tropic, fixed, and bipartite categories.</summary>
<keywords>Zodiac, Four Seasons, Elements, Ptolemy, Aries, Equinox, Solar Year, Generation and Corruption, Astrology</keywords>

--- Page 34 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the qualities of the zodiac signs, specifically their classification into tropical (movable), fixed, and bicorporeal (double-bodied) signs, following the previous section on the divisions of the year.</meta>

...the state is strengthened and made firm. The third is bipartite <note>double-bodied or mutable</note>, of which the first half belongs to that season, and the second verges toward the quality of the following one. Therefore, Aries, Cancer, Libra, and Capricorn are tropical <note>movable</note>. Taurus, Leo, Scorpio, and Aquarius are firm <note>fixed</note>. Gemini, Virgo, Sagittarius, and Pisces are bipartite <note>mutable</note>.

<margin>Chapter 7.</margin>

# ¶ Concerning the squares and circles of the movable, fixed, and bipartite signs.

<page-type>text</page-type>
<image-desc>A large decorative historiated or ornamental initial letter 'L' for the word 'LOCUS', set within a square frame featuring leafy scrollwork and floral motifs.</image-desc>

THE PLACE of the opinion of Hermes <note>Hermes Trismegistus, the legendary Hellenistic figure associated with the origins of astrology</note> following Abidemon <note>likely a corruption of a Greek name, possibly Agathodaemon</note> concerning the squares of the circle; the forms, number, and beginning of the signs; and the nature of the triplicities <note>groups of three signs belonging to the same element</note>. We bring his very words into the midst for the sake of completeness. "Since we know," he says, "that the universal partition is older than the particular, and that the beginning of things is approaching and increasing, while the end is receding and decreasing, the matter required us first to determine the number of the signs, and then to treat their natures. Since this should not be done without reason, a certain beginning for all things was necessary above all else." ¶ First, therefore, we mark out the entire circle equally with four equidistant points. When this is done, the two equinoctials first occur. One of these is passed at the end of Pisces; when the sun descends into the first degree of Aries, ascending in the circle, it brings the increase of the day. At the other, at the end of Virgo, the sun passing into Libra descends, bringing the increase of the night. Since we see that the ascent of the sun and the increase of light—the approach and growth of things—follow this, and the descent or retreat and decrease follow the other, we establish the beginning of the circle from Aries. Consequently, we distinguish the four seasons of the year through the four designated points of the circle. Through each individual season, there are three forms of the conjunctions of the lights <note>the Sun and Moon</note>, and three successions of opposite diversity: namely, the change of the day, the alternation of the path, and the alteration of the season. The change of the day is from increase to decrease and vice versa; the alternation of the path is from ascent to descent and vice versa; the alteration of the season is from the beginning to the middle, and from the middle to the end. Therefore, the form in which the sun enters and converts the season into another quality is called **tropical** <note>movable</note>. That in which it strengthens the state of the same season is **firm** <note>fixed</note>. For the **bipartite** <note>mutable</note>, the first half is of that season, and the following half is of the next. Since, therefore, three forms and three successions number each of the four quadrants, it was necessary that twelve be created from four times three. ¶ Having determined the number of the signs, it is then consequent to treat their nature. So that it may be done in order, we turn to the logic of the quadrants of the circle. Since the first of these is hot and moist; the second, hot and dry; the third, cold and dry; and the fourth, cold and moist. In the middle of the second, at the 15th degree of Leo, the strongest heat and greatest dryness are felt. Leo is therefore understood to be the hottest and driest of all, whose nature binds Aries and Sagittarius into the same triplicity by a shared kinship; and nothing is hotter or drier than fire. Therefore, Aries, Leo, and Sagittarius are **fiery**. Since, as with the four seasons, the first is hot and moist and the last is cold and moist, although the first triplicity is fiery, it was fitting for the last triplicity—that of Cancer, Scorpio, and Pisces—to possess a **watery** nature. In this manner, having examined Aries and Cancer and their triplicities, we pass to treating the nature of Taurus and Gemini. Since nothing can be hot and cold at the same time, nor can two...

<summary>This page explains the astronomical and seasonal rationale for the twelve zodiac signs, categorizing them into tropical, fixed, and mutable qualities based on the sun's path. It also begins to define the elemental triplicities, identifying the fiery nature of Aries, Leo, and Sagittarius.</summary>

--- Page 35 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the elemental qualities of the zodiac signs, specifically addressing the classification of signs by gender and by their relationship to day and night.</meta>

It is possible for things of the same nature to be contiguous. It followed that Taurus and its <term>trigon</term><note>A group of three signs belonging to the same element; in this case, the Earth triplicity.</note> should be cold and dry. With these being so divided, it is necessary that the fourth be left to the fourth. However, there are some students of this art who assume, based on a certain reasoning, that Aries ought rather to be hot and moist. For since the last season of the year is cold and moist, and the last quadrant of the day seems by necessity to follow the same, they argue that the first season of the year—the head of which is Aries—and the first quadrant of the day should rather be hot and moist as the head of the whole circle. We argue against these people from two points. First, because the order of the arrangement of the signs would indeed fit in that way if it began from the first season of the year and the first quadrant of the day. We, however, begin not from this, but from the middle of the second season and from midday in all <term>climates</term><note>Lat. "climatibus," referring to geographical zones or latitudes.</note>. The same is true through the movements of all the signs; the same sidereal order of nature is maintained. We begin from the day, for it was necessary to begin with that which agitates the movement of all things. Second, since moisture superabounds in the spring, if we saw that heat was less abundant in Aries, that point might be conceded. But since it is otherwise, the things already stated remain unvanquished.

### ¶ On Masculine and Feminine Signs. Chapter 8.

<image-desc>Decorative woodcut initial 'H' featuring floral motifs and a small human face at the top center of the letter's frame.</image-desc>
Having thoroughly examined these matters, we must next distinguish between masculine and feminine signs. For since whatever exists in this world is born conceived from the union of male and female, it was necessary that the power of the signs—which is the principle and cause of all generations—distinguish such a diversity of sexes among the signs. And because action belongs to the male and passion <note>In the classical sense of "passivity" or "being acted upon."</note> to the female, and among the elements the upper two (fire and air) are active, while the lower two (earth and water) are passive, it was fitting for the fiery and airy signs to be masculine, and the earthy and aquatic signs to be feminine. By this reasoning, Aries is masculine, Taurus is feminine, and likewise Gemini is masculine and Cancer is feminine, and so on through the order. There are, however, some who apply a distinction based on their rising: saying that the first rising sign is masculine, the second feminine, the third likewise masculine, and the fourth feminine, and so on. But we, because the distinction of sex in the nature of things is not accidental, derive it not through the accidental movement of the signs, but through the natural order.

### ¶ On Diurnal and Nocturnal Signs. Chapter 9.

<image-desc>Decorative woodcut initial 'P' with intricate foliage and scrollwork filling the square frame.</image-desc>
Finally, there is the distinction of the signs between day and night. Since the order of the signs, encircling the world in its daily revolution, alternates the vicissitudes of day and night—and since day is of a hot nature while night is cold, and day precedes while night follows, and they always accompany each other in turn without any middle gap—reason required that Aries be diurnal, Taurus nocturnal, Gemini likewise diurnal, and Cancer nocturnal, and so on through the order. There are, however, those who think that four signs (Aries, Cancer, Leo, and Sagittarius) are diurnal, the four opposite to these are nocturnal, and the remaining four are indifferent—that is, diurnal by day and nocturnal by night. Because they posit some masculine signs as diurnal and some feminine as nocturnal without any reasoning at all, they do not seem worthy of a response.

The author defends traditional assignments against alternative theories regarding the qualities of Aries and the sequence of the signs.</summary>
<keywords>astrology, zodiac signs, masculine and feminine signs, diurnal and nocturnal, four elements, Aries, Taurus, Gemini, Cancer, seasonal qualities</keywords>

->The third book contains nine chapters.<-

<image-desc>A large historiated woodcut initial 'P'. The scene within the letter shows two figures, possibly putti or angels, where one is carried on the shoulders of the other. First: why the seven stars <note>The seven "planets" of antiquity: Moon, Mercury, Venus, Sun, Mars, Jupiter, and Saturn</note> hold the leadership of common things above the others, and likewise concerning the effect of each in the four elements. ¶ 2nd: concerning the boundaries of <term>astrology</term> <note>In this period, "astrologia" often referred to both astronomy and astrology as we distinguish them today</note> and the astrologer. ¶ 3rd: concerning the property of the leadership of the sun in the tempering of the air and the composition of nature, and the participation of the stars with the sun. ¶ 4th: concerning the property of the leadership of the moon in the flow and ebb of the seas. ¶ 5th: concerning the cause of the flow and ebb. ¶ 6th: concerning the increase and decrease of the waters. ¶ 7th: that the flow and ebb of the sea belongs to the moon rather than the nature of the sea: confirming this from four places. ¶ 8th: concerning the leadership of the moon in the seas and the form of those things which flow and ebb and those which do not, and the effect of the sun in the air. ¶ 9th: concerning the leadership of the moon in animals, seeds, and metals, and their increase and movement.

### Concerning the fixed stars and the planets. Chapter I.

<image-desc>The same historiated woodcut initial 'P' is repeated here.</image-desc>
Beyond the whole number of the stars, it has been demonstrated that the 12 signs <note>The Zodiac</note> obtain the leadership of common things above the rest; thus it seems necessary to explain for what reason, out of the entire multitude of stars, the seven planets are allotted the leadership of general things above the others. For there are those who wonder why, since the other stars share so much kinship of nature and influence with these seven—for they move from sign to sign, rise and set over the lower world, and also become Oriental and Occidental with various complexions—why the leadership of things should prefer these seven as if the others did nothing. To this, the entire reasoning of the ancient authority concerning the effect of the stars plainly responds. For since whatever is born and dies in this world follows the signs and stars as an efficient cause, and since the order of the signs (as approved in the second book) presides over the nature of the elements, it follows that the stars disposed through those signs preside over the generation of those same elements. The difference is that because these seven, running through those signs more swiftly and with varied paths, go and return more frequently, they are more prompt in the movements and effects of things. Hence, while the general leadership of things proceeds to these seven, the others are by no means immune to the office of private things according to their own measure. This is also shown by the fact that among those wandering stars, the more any one is drawn by a swifter and more varied motion, the more it is found to have a greater office in the effect of things. Hence it is that the Moon, the swiftest of all, is more frequently occupied among all the effects of things. For the <term>fixed stars</term> <note>stellae firmae; the stars that do not move relative to one another</note> lead to the private, stable, or slow properties of individual things. Furthermore, while the celestial circle with all the stars encompasses this world in a perpetual circuit, the stable stars are carried through that same circle with almost the same slow motion and the same distance from the globe of the earth; the seven, however, possess much diversity, being much swifter, each in its own circle and its own diverse motion—such as a straight path, now a station, now retrogradation, ascent, descent, increase of course, and decrease. And just as the course of such planets never ceases, so the world never ceases from the generation, corruption, and alteration of things. It is understood—

<summary>This page introduces Book III, outlining nine chapters focused on planetary influence, particularly the Sun and Moon. The first chapter explains why the seven planets are considered the primary drivers of earthly change compared to the "fixed" stars due to their swift and varied motions.</summary>
<keywords>astrology, seven planets, twelve signs, fixed stars, Moon, Sun, elements, stellar motion, generation and corruption, elemental nature</keywords>

--- Page 37 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of celestial influences on the physical world, transitioning from the elemental nature of the planets to the formal definitions of astrology and the role of the astrologer.</meta>

So great is the variety of accidents <note>In medieval philosophy, "accidents" are non-essential properties of a thing, such as its color or temporary state, as opposed to its essence.</note> in the world and so great the diversity of the movements of the planets: it is rightly more desirable that they should not unjustly be seen to obtain, by a certain privilege, the leadership of common things, as the distinct qualities of each are appropriated to their natural element. For the **Sun** and **Mars** are inclined toward a fiery nature; **Saturn** and **Mercury** toward an earthy nature; **Jupiter** and the **Head** <note>The "Head" refers to the North Node of the Moon, Caput Draconis.</note> toward an airy nature; **Venus**, the **Moon**, and the **Tail** <note>The "Tail" refers to the South Node of the Moon, Cauda Draconis.</note> toward an aquatic nature. However, there is a certain distinction among these simple categories: for although the Sun and Mars are both hot and dry, the heat of the Sun is vital, while that of Mars is destructive. Likewise, the cold and dryness of Mercury is a favorable nature, while that of Saturn is hostile; the temperament of Jupiter is a nourishment to nature, while that of the Head, though complex, is not sufficiently friendly; Venus and the Moon are also favorable to birth, while the Tail is less suitable.

## On the definitions of astrology and the astrologer. Chapter 2.

<image-desc>Ornamental woodcut initial 'H' featuring a figure in a robe, possibly a scholar or clerical figure, seated within a decorative architectural frame.</image-desc>

THESE things having been established, since the science of such celestial virtues <note>original: "virtuū," referring to the inherent powers or influences of the stars.</note> seems almost infinite, it is appropriate to determine the matter through art and to prescribe limits for the practitioner, so that from both, the end of each may be understood more easily and certainly. So that this may be done reasonably, we shall first distinguish them by their names; for the name of **Astrology** adorns the science, while the name of **Astrologer** adorns the learned man. **Astrology**, therefore, is the science of the virtues of stellar motion toward a defined time and toward the consequence of that time. It is a science, for the genus of astrology differs from others of its kind; for we commemorate the powers of stellar motion as those which concede leadership to nothing outside themselves—just as lightning and thunder are signs of rain, yet are not the rain itself; smoke is from fire, but is not the fire itself, and so on. In this manner, the movements of the stars lead to the effects of things far distant from themselves. Therefore, we speak of a "defined time and its consequence" because the more skillful astrologer first arrives at the beginning of things in their proper time, and then measures the end of things. 

The **Astrologer**, however, is the one who demonstrates wisdom regarding the sidereal power and the virtues of the stars. Since the individual movements of the stars and constellations provide leadership for all the accidents of this world, and yet human ingenuity and study are hardly sufficient to grasp the entirety of every individual thing, let us distinguish the leadership of the stars by three limits. 

**First**, indeed, there are things or acts under sidereal leadership so subtle and profound that human faculty is sufficient neither to follow the leadership of the stars themselves nor to arrive at where they lead. 

**Second**, we follow the sidereal leadership up to the thing itself, but we fail before the quantities are measured and the qualities are grasped. 

**Third**, there are things and acts which we follow through the celestial leadership, including both their quantities and qualities. 

Thus, regarding the first limit: such as the integrity of all species under all genera, and of all individual things under whatever division—like the number of the sands of the sea or the pebbles of the field; or the daily and momentary measure of the increase or decrease of individual bodies; or the difference of all movements and accidents through individual instances. For although individuals are of the same genus and even of one species, they differ by three intervals: as when two or more individuals differ in whiteness or blackness, in length or shortness, in a flat-nosed face or some color of the eyes, in the width or narrowness of the mouth, in the scent of the breath, in the health or roughness of mind and body, and similar things—of which there is no general or special difference held. Therefore, in the individual intervals of all things, although the stars...

<summary>This page defines the elemental natures of the planets and nodes, distinguishing between beneficial and destructive influences. It further defines astrology as a science of celestial motion and outlines the limitations of human knowledge in predicting specific individual details.</summary>
<keywords>astrology, astrologer, planetary elements, four elements, celestial influence, limits of knowledge, lunar nodes, natural philosophy</keywords>

--- Page 38 ---
<meta>This page continues the philosophical defense of astrology, transitioning from the theoretical limits of human knowledge to the specific practical structure of the art and the influence of the Sun.</meta>

...provide a clear path, yet it is not within the scope of human faculty to pursue all things. ¶ The failure of the second limit is that we might have a certain number among the populations of two or more cities—even if each part is guided by the stars—just as we have a determined number of grains of grain in a field, or the lengths and widths of deserted islands. In these and similar cases, although we hold that the guidance of the stars accounts for their quantity and qualities, our faculty is not sufficient to attain them; all these things, because they are [beyond] the power of human nature, are necessary neither for human use nor for the astrologer. ¶ The faculty of the third limit is the certain guidance of the stars toward the genera and species of human invention, the alterations of the seasons of the year, the movements of the world's elements, and the resolutions, generations, and corruptions of things, as well as the quantities and qualities of human use and many similar things. To be brief, I say that the diverse movements and powers of the stars and constellations are the guide to the individual accidents <note>In philosophy, an "accident" is a non-essential attribute of a thing, such as its color or temporary state.</note> of all things. Truly, a part of this kind of guidance remains constant in matters of human intellect, while a part exceeds it in matters so subtle and profound that they withdraw into impossibility. Because these things have escaped man, the investigation of them is not necessary for the astrologer. For example: it is a constant fact that the Sun’s passage and return through the quadrants of the circle <note>The four seasons or the cardinal points of the zodiac.</note> bring back different times and affect the elements with various movements; but while it is certain that the usual movements of the seasons follow the solar steps through individual signs or degrees, it is foreign to human intellect to determine whether the Sun's motion—be it its own or circular—causes varieties of times or elements, or affects animals or seeds, by a single second, third, or tenth [of a degree]; and so it is with the other stars. ¶ Having thus determined both the nature of the art and the duties of the practitioner, finally all six circumstances of this craft must be arranged. Namely: the **Beginning**, the **Root**, the **Branches**, the **Argument**, the **Fruit**, and the **End**. Therefore, the **Beginning** of the art is the study of the knowledge of how things come forth; the **Root** is the knowledge of celestial movements. The **Branches** are the judgments of imminent movements of things derived from the movements of celestial bodies. The **Argument** is the customary effect of the judgments. The **Fruit** is the providence <note>Foreknowledge or preparation.</note> of resulting things. The **End** of this kind of providence is utility.

## ¶ Chapter Three.

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration depicting the Sun and Moon with human faces in the sky. Below them, a man lies upon a rectangular plinth or bed. To the right, a standing figure in draped robes holds a staff or caduceus. On the far right is the astrological symbol for the Sun (a circle with a center dot).</image-desc>

Now, beginning with the Sun, we shall treat its properties, the tempering of nature and composition, and also the participation of the stars with the Sun <note>original: "☉"</note>. In this part, it seems we must first respond to those who [attribute] the tempering of nature and the composition of things to the Sun...

<summary>The author defines the limits of astrological knowledge, distinguishing between what is theoretically possible and what is useful for humans, before outlining the six-part structure of the astrological craft and beginning a chapter on the Sun's influence.</summary>
<keywords>astrology, limits of knowledge, solar influence, elements, seasons, methodology, providence, celestial movements</keywords>

--- Page 39 ---
<meta>This page continues a philosophical and astronomical argument regarding the influence of celestial bodies on the generation and corruption of earthly matter, likely following a discussion on the elements.</meta>

Those who deny the power of the sun and the stars <note>original: "stellarumqꝫ virtuti"</note> believe that things coalesce by some power inherent to their own substance. They are no less in error than those who, while admitting that the composition of things changes due to external causes, nevertheless deny this power to the stars alone. First, therefore, one must counter them by noting that nothing composed is ever composed without a composer. Indeed, it is impossible for anything to compose itself. If it were so, no element would be resolved into another, but each would always remain unchanged within itself. However, we see that they are resolved into one another, as is found in composite things; for nothing resolves itself. 

Likewise, if things were not moved from elsewhere but generated themselves, it would surely follow that a thing would end from the same source it was born. For nothing is so contrary to its own nature that it would destroy itself. Since this is absurd, it is clear that other causes for the generation and corruption of things exist outside of themselves, which are suited to the receptive nature of things. 

Next, I shall say that it follows that God, the maker of all things, established the Sun <note>The symbol $\odot$ is used throughout for the Sun.</note> and the stars as efficient causes for the tempering of natures and the composition of things. For just as fire burns by its own inherent power and is the cause of scorching, so the divine light of the Sun—the universal author of light and heat—exists as the efficient cause of the natural composition of things by a divinely granted power. 

The effect itself proves this. For those regions of the earth where the Sun either approaches more than is equal or stays too far away remain entirely sterile of both living creatures and seeds. For example: those places on earth distant from the summer tropic <note>The Tropic of Cancer</note> toward the north by 66 degrees—which number is reached by adding the Sun's total deviation of nearly 24 degrees to 90—these places, I say, being far from the Sun's path, are locked in perpetual and certain frost; they deny all nourishment to both seeds and animals. For from the time the Sun descends into the southern signs for six whole months, those places lack its rising; hence, exposed to the full force of the north winds, they remain frozen and unyielding in both summer and winter. 

This is confirmed by the Armenian Sea, which, being 21 degrees removed from the Tropic of Cancer <note>symbol: $\sigma$</note>, is so burning with violent winds and blinded by the obscurity of the air that it never permits ships. Although it is much closer, the winter wandering of the Sun renders it intractable. The northern boundaries of the herds <note>likely referring to the "Armenti" or nomadic regions</note> bear witness to this. As long as the Sun walks through the southern signs, they remain buried under a single mass and do not appear for six whole months; so that within those six months, many of their animals perish from intolerable cold, and many birds remain shut up in their nests for four whole months for fear of the cold, cut off from the middle of the world. This happens even though they are not much more than 15 degrees distant from the equinoctial line. 

Otherwise, upon the sea, from the beginning of Scorpio <note>symbol: $\mkern-3mu\lower.1ex\unicode{x264F}\mkern-3mu$</note> to the head of Pisces <note>symbol: $\unicode{x2653}$</note>, the absence of the Sun and the succeeding blast of cold deny navigation. The same occurs to the north and the south; indeed, within 20 degrees of the equinoctial line, near the Sun's path, the heat is so intolerable and the places so quickly scorched that no nourishment for animals or seeds is left. From the fifth degree of Leo <note>symbol: $\mkern-3mu\lower.1ex\unicode{x264D}\mkern-3mu$</note> to the opposite point in Pisces <note>symbol: $\unicode{x2653}$</note>, the solar passage over those places is a continuous heat <note>original: "thaurorꝫ", likely a corruption of "fervor"</note>. Everything beneath the earth is sterile with dryness; the sands among which the Nile is hidden, and the Ethiopian Sea, permit neither navigation nor life. Because the Sun rises so closely above, it draws out the finer parts of the water by a natural "tincture" <note>evaporation</note>. Thus, what remains, having conceived such a powerful heat, is so dense and bitter that it remains stagnant and intractable. Hence, the regions in the middle, between the Sun's approaches...

and its withdrawal, for nature leaves behind a suitable balance once the food and nourishment have been arranged. ¶ From these things, therefore, it is clear that if the Sun <note>original: $\odot$</note> were raised up to the ninth sphere, or lowered down to the lunar orb, the world could not stand, either from the cold in the former case or the excessive heat in the latter. For this reason, the provident author of all things, God, placed the Sun in the middle region of the world as a universal kindler of bodily life. He also found in the habitable part of the earth itself good places which, as they approach or recede from the Sun, are varied with much difference. For instance, in those parts which are more remote from the solar circuit than others, they are overcome by much snow and powerful cold; because much moisture desires flesh, but cold produces whiteness and a fleshy habit. These people differ from others by their hair, which is brought forth by the heat of the whiteness, and by a hostile spirit and a dull wit. The Ethiopians, however, or Moors <note>historical terms for people of Sub-Saharan and North Africa</note>, because they inhabit those parts of the earth over which the Sun is carried in Aries, Leo, and Sagittarius <note>original: $\gamma$ $\delta$ $\pi$ (symbols for the fiery signs of the zodiac)</note>, excel others in black color, curly hair, a thin habit, weak memory, and a light spirit, due to the excessive heat and dryness prevailing in their nature. But those in the middle, such as those of the fourth and fifth climates <note>term for geographical zones or latitudes</note> and those contiguous to them, excel others in divination; as those who enjoy a moderate approach and withdrawal of the Sun, they are of a temperate nature: with a suitable habit, beautiful color, a nimble spirit, subtle wit, and sound memory. Although they generally share in these properties, there are also certain special differences among them according to the diversity of places, to such an extent that even between individual cities and provinces, though near and contiguous, there is no small difference in the form of men as well as in their morals, habit, and custom, as much for the approach and withdrawal of the Sun as for the nature and habit of the fixed stars rising over them. But these very properties vary each year between increases and decreases and other alterations; since this was discovered to be found here neither from the Sun nor from any stars by their own special motion or gaze, it was found that the causes of such variations are the meetings of the Sun now with the wandering stars <note><term>wandering stars</term>: the planets</note> and now with the fixed ones, and their mixtures with one another. From this it was understood that the Sun and stars together compose the nature of things and share in the division of genera and species; except that the Sun is principally in charge of the nature of living things and the generations of life, seeds, and metals. The stars, however, share more in the private morals and tenor of individual provinces; they do, however, temper the air: for the illumination of the air belongs to the lights, whose rising they follow by approaching, and whose setting they follow by receding. Just as the Sun heats, purges, and thins the air, so do the Moon <note>original: $\leftmoon$</note> and the stars in their own way. Whence Hippocrates <note>original: Ypocras</note> says in the *Book of Climates*: "Unless the Moon and stars thinned the nocturnal density, the elements would corrupt the life of all bodies with the thickness of impenetrable air." ¶ For these reasons, therefore, since the Sun alone would suffice to seek the universal necessities of all things, God the Creator associated the stars as ministers to the solar office, communicating with a secondary power. ¶ Since, therefore, the passage of the Sun through the twelve signs is the cause of the alteration of the seasons, and the alteration of the seasons is the cause of elementary resolution, and finally the resolution is the cause of all generations and corruptions, it follows that the same solar passage through those signs is the cause of all things which accompany the motion of the Sun, distinguished at certain times. Some indeed in years, others in months, others in days: as in spring the grasses, herbs, and leaves of trees and many animals; in summer the grains of corn and the fruits of trees; in autumn the corruption of the spring things; in winter the corruption of the summer generations. Since these continue at distinct times, they also follow distinct solar locations. Those things, therefore, which do not...

In all years, all things follow with the same quality and quantity, since the Sun <note>original: "☉"</note> returns through the same places each year; the cause is the participation of the stars with the Sun. For if the Sun alone administered all things by itself, then winter would not differ from winter, nor summer from summer, nor autumn from autumn. However, those things which are administered through the world in a daily succession—such as the daily conceptions, births, and growth of humans and animals—bind themselves more to the universal circuit of the entire upper world, following it as it agitates this lower world by its natural motion.

### ¶ On the property of the Moon's leadership in the flow and ebb of the seas. Chapter 4.

After the airy tempering of the solar effect, there follows the leadership of the Moon <note>original: "☽"</note> in the movement of waters and the alternating flow and ebb of the seas. As is well known among philosophers <note>original: "phōs" (philosophoros)</note>, the two lights divide the order of the four elements <term>elements: the classical building blocks of matter—fire, air, water, and earth</term> in this way: the Sun leads fire and air, while the Moon leads earth and water. For two reasons, the leadership of the lights in this world is both stronger and more manifest than that of the stars. First, regarding the Sun: the Sun is indeed the greatest of all stars. The Moon, however, is the smallest of all celestial bodies and the closest to the earth. Second, because while light is in the stars, it is not as effective as rays; their effect is more in their motion. In the lights, however, the rays are not a little effective in this world, transmitting the collected forces of the upper world to the lower world through their rays. For even Hippocrates <note>original: "Ypocras"</note> asserts that the Moon, as the mediatrix between the celestial bodies and the bodies of the lower world, transfers their forces by mediating between them. Therefore, just as the Sun prevails in the tempering of nature and the composition of things, so the Moon has a more effective power in the movements of waters, the state and accidents of bodies, sprouts, fruits, odors, and things of that kind. We begin the enumeration of these from the alternating flow and ebb of the seas; as their increases and decreases are diverse, so they generate diverse opinions among nations. For there are those who assert the flow lasts from the separation of the lights <note>the New Moon</note> until their opposition <note>the Full Moon</note>, and the ebb from there until their meeting. It is certain to all, however, that even on individual days, from the rising of the Moon, the sea for which it rises follows its flow until it reaches the mid-heaven of that region, and from there follows its ebb until its setting. This is the daily experience of the Indians and Persians in the sea that approaches Ethiopia, as well as in the islands of the oceanic hemispheres. The alternating spaces of the flows and ebbs succeeding one another continuously are of this sort: for whichever sea the Moon first emerges to, its flow begins immediately, following it by increasing until the Moon reaches the meridian line <note>original: "celi cardinem"</note>; when it transcends that line, turning toward the west, the ebb succeeds, accompanying it by decreasing until it sets. Or, when it sets, the flow immediately begins its journey again, increasing until the Moon reaches the bottom of the earth <note>the nadir</note>; as the Moon recedes from there, the ebb likewise succeeds until it reaches the east. Thus, there occur individual...

<meta>This page continues a discussion on natural philosophy, specifically focusing on the mechanical and celestial causes of the tides. The previous context likely established the spherical nature of the Earth and the Moon's influence on terrestrial elements.</meta>

In those days, there are twin inflows <note>original: "accessus"; the rising tide</note> and twin outflows <note>original: "recessus"; the ebbing tide</note> at times considered to be the daily ☽ course. This variety occurs through different parts of the seas, which are subject to different poles <note>original: "cardinibus"; literally 'hinges' or 'pivots', referring here to the cardinal points of the lunar orbit</note> of the lunar circuit. For since the smoky <note>likely referring to the misty or vaporous atmosphere</note> orb of the sea surrounds the spherical Earth, so the daily lunar circuits ☽ circle around it. Always ☽ in some parts of both the seas and the lands, the moon is at one pole while simultaneously being at another for other parts; thus, at the same hour and every moment, it is in the east for some, setting for others, at the highest point of heaven for some, and at the pole of the earth <note>the nadir, or the point directly below</note> for others. From this, it follows that at the same moment, there is an inflow of the sea for some and an outflow for others. However, this does not happen in one single way for everyone. Even those who meanwhile furrow the deep open sea <note>original: "pelagus"</note> with a ship feel the beginning of the inflow as a certain boiling of the waves, which, bubbling up from the bottom and stirring up stormy blasts by the powerful impulse of the breezes, saddens the whole sea; they feel the outflow by the relaxation of this. It is much different for those who meanwhile dwell upon the shores. For they do not experience this kind of blast or boiling, but only a swelling of the waters; a certain inundation reaches them. So great is the diversity of these movements between those places that some do not even believe this to be the cause of the movements. For the inflow does not begin except in a deep and wide place containing much dense water, with a rough and mountainous bottom, and close to the lunar path. Whence, when they find the rising moon above them in its natural speed, it first pushes the nearby waters; and the impulse of the waters goes out little by little until it floods the furthest shores, which it was impossible to reach at the exact moment the movement began.

## ¶ On the cause of the inflow and outflow. Chapter 5.

<image-desc>Ornamental woodcut initial 'H' containing an urn with stylized flowers and leafy foliage, set within a rectangular frame with a decorative border.</image-desc>
Having established these things, it is fitting to examine the causes of the inflows and outflows. We say, therefore, that an inflow and outflow of this kind is never produced except by the meeting of three things: namely, the nature of the place, the quality of the waters, and the movement of the ☽. The nature of the place is that the location of the waters be deep, long, and wide—hardly to be crossed in a short expense of time—mountainous, rough, and hard, so that by any light movement, the many waters are sharply struck back and conceive swelling waves. The quality of the waters is that such a great amount of water is gathered in such a long place from the temperate climate that it is not felt to increase or decrease by the flow of rivers or the gathering of springs. These waters, having been thickened over time and heated by the salt, stir up dense vapors which, mixed with the vapors of the earth, breathe life into the waves to be stirred. Moreover, the movement of the rising and setting moon from above, repeated quite often, draws waters of this kind by a kindred power; for following the pull of its own accord as far as the moon approaches, they approach until, boiling over and being less contained in their own place, they flood the furthest shores. For a place that is tender, soft, and flat is not suitable for this because of the running of the waters; nor are running waters like rivers and streams suitable because of their thinness, being less fit for dense vapors. The cause, however, from the setting ☽ down to the pole of the earth, just as from the east to the highest pole, is followed by a triple inflow. First, because the lines of the east and west are equidistant; however much is taken from the rising to the summit, just as much is equidistant from the west to the pole of the earth. Thus, the whole eastern quadrant yields to the whole eastern [influence] and is equidistant; and ☽ both are approaching. Second, because in the climate of the world, the rising of the signs through the poles of heaven and earth...

<meta>This page continues a discussion on the relationship between the Moon's movement and the tides, specifically focusing on the calculation of tidal hours and the influence of the celestial spheres.</meta>

The circles of the right sphere are equal to the orbits. For just as the rising and setting, so also the lines of the cardinal hours are equally distant. Whence, in whatever degree number a sign may rise, it is necessary that its exact opposite should set. Thirdly, it follows that the approach <note>original: "accessus"</note> of the Moon is always from the horizon to the mid-heaven, while its withdrawal <note>original: "recessus"</note> is from the middle of the circle toward the east. Thus, the setting of the Moon is similar to the horizon, and the hinge of the earth <note>the meridian</note> is similarly the meeting point of the middle; so that in the eastern quadrant there is an approach, and in the western quadrant a withdrawal, and thus in the other two quadrants, the lunar approaches and withdrawals accompany one another. There are, however, some who nevertheless see such approaches and withdrawals in certain fresh waters, as in certain maritime cities of Ethiopia, and some of Gaul or Germany, whom the proximity of the sea deceives. For indeed, the rivers flowing into the sea and those contiguous with the marine waves, being repulsed by the overflowing approach of the sea, seem to approach like a full Alpheus <note>A reference to the river Alpheus, which in mythology was said to flow under the sea without mixing its waters</note> gliding in. Moreover, the approach of the water is warmer and the withdrawal colder. For in the approach, they bubble up from the deepest abysses; in the withdrawal, having been spread out externally, they cool down. In the approach, therefore, the waters accompany the Moon; in the withdrawal, even if they do not accompany the Moon naturally, they return to the seas from whence they came. It is established that naturally the Moon, both above the earth and under the earth, matches the following withdrawal to the preceding approach in an equal space of time. But because the arc of the Moon above the earth is never equal to the one below the earth, those approaches and withdrawals which are above the earth can never be equal to those below the earth. Whenever, therefore, it pleases one to measure the hours of approach or withdrawal of the upper hemisphere, the first degree with which the Moon rises must be noted, then that with which it sets, not neglecting its latitude; then one must take the distance from the degree of rising to the degree of setting through the rising of that climate, and divide the whole by fifteen degrees <note>15 degrees equals one hour of time in celestial movement</note>. Whatever the division is, that will be the number of equal hours of the whole time of both spaces. If anything less than 15 remains, it is a fraction of an hour. Half of the whole number is the space of time for either the natural approach or withdrawal while the Moon occupies the upper hemisphere. For under the earth, by reversing from the degree of setting to the degree of rising, the subterranean arc—namely the approach and withdrawal of the lower part—takes its duration through that space. He who studies to grasp these things more certainly should have a fixed latitude of the earth and a fixed rising of the signs in that latitude.

### ¶ On the Increase and Decrease of Waters. Chapter 6.

Among the things said above, it was asserted that naturally the durations of the approach and withdrawal of each hemisphere are equal in their respective parts. Now it must be discussed how accidentally, at times, some inequality intervenes. For inequality is seen when the approach and withdrawal of one hemisphere are measured in an equal quantity of time, yet each is equalized simultaneously with the space of the other's time; so that by as much as the approach of the upper hemisphere becomes longer or shorter in duration than the following withdrawal, by so much the approach of the lower hemisphere becomes longer or shorter than the upper withdrawal. For the inequality of this duration between increases and decreases is about one hour, more or less; so that if there is a great abundance of waters, the duration of the approach may increase by a little more or less than an hour over the number of the withdrawal, and similarly in the contrary case. ¶ This entire inequality, therefore, is derived from eight factors. The first indeed is the distance of the Moon and the Sun, and the increase of light...

<meta>This page continues the discussion of lunar influence on the tides, specifically detailing the eight factors or "places" that govern the increase and decrease of the seas.</meta>

7 decrease. Second, the position of the moon's rectitude <note>original: "rectitudinis"; likely referring to the moon's corrected motion or anomaly</note> when an addition or subtraction is made to its mean course. In the third place, the moon's position in the eccentric circle. In the fourth, its place in the circle of deviation <note>the lunar latitude or inclination</note>. Fifth, its position between the south and the north. Sixth, the days which the Egyptians and Westerners call "marine days," which are days of the increase and decrease of the waters—a factor which is not strictly a property of the moon itself. Seventh, the length or shortness of the day or night, which is a property of the sun. In the eighth place is how much the force of the winds contributes. There are, therefore, four distinct places between the increases and decreases of the moon relative to the sun. First is the moon's conjunction with the sun. Second is the first quadrature <term>tetragonus: a square aspect of 90 degrees</term> when it is at a diagonal. Third is the opposition <note>the Full Moon</note> where the light of both luminaries is greatest. Fourth is the second quadrature. Therefore, at the conjunction of the luminaries, the flow <term>accessus: the incoming tide</term> of the sea is powerful and the ebb <term>recessus: the outgoing tide</term> is extensive; for the sun, joined with the moon in these phases, adds its strength. Indeed, the sun also possesses some power over the flow of the sea, just as it does whenever the moon is joined with "watery" stars <note>planets or constellations traditionally associated with moisture, like Venus or Pisces</note>. At the hour when the moon, joined with the sun, sinks low—as if conceiving the seeds of things from the outpouring of nature—it immediately demonstrates the strength of that conception in the movement of the subject element, that is, the sea, by a manifest touch. For the moon is far more effective when with the sun than when with the stars, due to the natural privilege of the moon's meeting with the sun. From this hour of conjunction, as much as the moon recedes, the force of the flow decreases and the ebb increases until the first quadrature. Here, the decrease having ended, the turn is made and the flow increases until it reaches the full moon. From here until the next quadrature there is a decrease, and from there until the conjunction again, there is a lessening of the ebb and an increase of the flow. Except that in the opposition—that is, the full moon—the force of the moon is more effective, like one bringing forth mature conceptions for the first time; and likewise in the first and second quadratures. Therefore, just as within the revolution of the world (that is, one day) there are twin flows and twin ebbs following the daily progress of the moon, so within the moon's return (that is, one month) there are twin decreases: the former occurring between the cardinal points of the sky, the latter between the cardinal points of the moon relative to the sun. In the second place, the "first moon" <note>the new moon or the start of the lunar cycle</note> must be positioned. While the rectitude of the moon is added to its mean, the force of the flow prevails; and while it is subtracted, the force of the ebb is more powerful, governed by the quantity of that rectitude. However, when nothing is added or subtracted, the force of neither the flow nor the ebb increases or decreases in this regard. These movements are also followed by fresh waters, rivers, and springs, which follow these lunar leads. In the third place, as long as the moon is within 200 degrees from the apogee <note>original: "abside"; the highest point of its orbit</note> of its circle, the force of the flow prevails; within 90 degrees, the ebb. In the fourth place, as long as the moon ascends in its latitude, the force of the flow increases; as long as it descends, the ebb increases. In the fifth place, as long as the moon travels through northern signs, it increases the flow of the northern seas and the ebb of the southern; but while it is in the southern signs, the situation is reversed: the flow of the southern seas prevails and the ebb of the northern, as far as this category is concerned. This is especially true when, after the first quadrature, the moon adds to the calculation and is in the lower part of its circle from its rising; for when the moon is in watery signs with watery and descending stars—all these being united—not only do the flows of the seas abound, but even rivers and springs increase. In the sixth place are the lunar days of the month which we call "marine days." For the month of a lunation consists of 29 and nearly a half days, which are divided into four parts...
