The quadrants of the angles are completed in seven and nearly a half days. Therefore, from the 27th day of the month until the fourth and a half day of the following month, they are called "diminishing" <note>Latin: *diminutim*; referring to the waning phase or decreasing tidal force</note>; the following four and a half days are "increasing." Because of this, the tides feel certain losses in the former and increases in the latter. Following this motion, the two remaining quadrants succeed each other in turn between these two movements up to 27 days. This was discovered in this manner by the Egyptians and the Westerners. To all the Easterners skilled in the conditions of the sea, it appeared that the whole of these quadrants altered through increases and decreases in this way: but among the seven days which they call "diminutions," one or two are days of loss, and among the "augmentations," just as many are days of increase. It is established, however, among the people of Memphis <note>The ancient Egyptian capital</note> that between these quadrants of the lunations <term>lunationum: the period from one new moon to the next</term> there are also movements of the higher fresh waters between the increases and decreases. Some of the seafaring folk, counting the days of the lunations between increases and decreases in a threefold way, feel an increase of the tide <term>accessus: the incoming tide or flood</term> in the first 7 or 10 days, a decrease in the last 10, and a middle state in the middle days. In the seventh place is how much the Sun <note>original: ☉</note> also adds support to these kinds of movements with its powers. For although the Moon <note>original: ☽</note> is indeed the closer leader of the tides and ebbs <term>recessuum: the outgoing tide</term>, nevertheless the Sun as well as the stars add something to their increases and decreases. For it has been found through various seas in which these movements appear that at certain times of the year, between day and night, the mutual force of these increases and decreases is altered, which seems to happen because of the Sun between the southern and northern signs. For as long as the day is longer than the night, the daytime tide of the sea prevails over the nocturnal one; and similarly in reverse. Therefore, since it pertains to this class of movements, an equality of both is felt at the equinoctial points, but at the solstitial points, the maximum increase of one and decrease of the other is felt.

¶ There is a double cause for this alteration of these movements. The first is how much the sun adds during the successions of day and night. For when the day is longer than the night, the sun, heating the waters above the earth for a longer time, calls forth the waves from the lowest abysses by the powers of heat; whence it is necessary that the daytime movements of the waters prevail over the nocturnal ones. In the second place is how much the longer delay of the moon in the upper hemisphere <term>hemisphærium: the half of the celestial sphere above the horizon</term> then adds; for when the night is longer than the day, the moon is above the earth at night, and because it retains the rising signs longer, it makes the nocturnal movements surpass the daytime ones by the space of its delay. Hence, therefore, just as there are two ebbs for each individual day, so it was said above that two increases and two decreases of both occur for each individual month, just as now; and it appears that just as many increases and decreases of those movements occur for each individual year, according to a certain similarity agreeing with them. For as the nocturnal tide of the moon above the earth with the sun being in Sagittarius seems to imitate the likeness of their conjunction tides, so the daytime tide of the opposition [imitates it] with the sun in Gemini and the moon occupying the upper hemisphere; and thus the others are easily measured concerning the other annual and monthly increments, though not with equality. Therefore, if all these testimonies should ever coincide, powerful and very vast increases of the waters will follow, and the measure of their quantity succeeds according to that number. These, therefore, are the 7 natural leaders. For the eighth is accidental, namely how much [is added] to the movements of the waters...

<summary>This page explains the division of the lunar month into quadrants that dictate tidal increases and decreases, while also detailing how the Sun's heat and the seasonal length of days (solstices and equinoxes) influence water levels.</summary>
<keywords>tides, lunar cycles, solar influence, equinox, solstice, Egyptian astronomy, Memphis, hydrography</keywords>

--- Page 46 ---
Great winds, driven across the sea, stir up the entire ocean. There are two types of winds that agitate the whole sea: one type arises from the depths of the sea where the upper motion is reflected back by the rocky bottom; these burst forth with force toward the surface from the lowest abysses, as was said before, just as occurs in rivers. The other type is driven through the breezes of both land and sea, rushing down from above; meeting the waters of the sea, they stir up the furious motions of the deep. The first type, passing only through the boundaries of the twin seas <note>likely referring to the two hemispheres or specific local bodies of water</note>, rarely reach the shores. The blasts of the second type, however, blowing from diverse places, fly across the globe. For some, their primary locations are fixed; for others, they are secondary. The primary ones are at the four cardinal points of the world <note>North, South, East, and West</note>. The secondary ones are midway between these four.

In these matters, as far as this business is concerned, it must be primarily noted that since the ebb of the sea is committed to the motion of the moon from east to west: the eastern winds favor the strength of the incoming tide <term>accessus</term>, while the opposing western winds promote the strength of the receding tide <term>recessus</term>. Thus, among the southern and northern winds, each breathing from its own region, they obstruct their opposites. Because it was said above that the natural incoming tide of each hemisphere is equal in time to the following receding tide, that remains true unless some inequality happens by chance, as has been mentioned. There are two types of this "accidental" inequality: one is intrinsic, for which we have described seven causes; the other is extrinsic, namely that which the force of the winds adds.

From these eight factors, therefore, every increase and decrease of the tides is varied. Regarding these incoming and receding tides, the universal opinion is this: the incoming tide follows the first motion by the natural effect of the moon. The receding tide, however, is the natural return of the waters to the seas from whence they had gone out. Therefore, when the duration of the incoming tide is extended longer, the space of the following receding tide is shortened by roughly the amount of that increase. The reverse happens similarly. Furthermore, no small amount of inequality arises sometimes from the difficulty of the shorelines. For when the incoming tide floods the outer shores, if it happens to flow into hollows of rocks or valleys or deep pits, it is necessary that less returns from the part left behind. So also, where the incoming tide strives against rivers flowing into the sea, it is necessary that the receding tide prevails due to the fresh waters.

### ¶ Chapter Seven.

<image-desc>A large decorative woodcut initial 'H' containing a stylized human face with long, flowing hair or rays, set within a rectangular frame with ornate floral and scrollwork patterns.</image-desc>

Having explained these things, I think the cause of the moon must now be asserted in this way: that it may be left as settled if we weaken the opinion of those who contradict us. For they say that it is inherent in the nature of the sea—and not by any power of the moon—that it stirs the waves with a certain single boiling heat, and that the incoming tide occurs as these flow down. <note>The author is presenting the counter-argument that tides are caused by internal "boiling" rather than lunar gravity.</note> For if [the moon] drives the tide of the sea by surrounding it from above, why does it not also stir the waters of all springs by the same causes and with the same motions?

To these people we respond from four points. First, indeed, if no foreign force but the vigor of its own nature administered the tide of the sea, it would never take on any inequality, but would always occur in the same way, at the same time, and with the same quantity of space. For something that is its own, and not suffering any foreign work, would never depart from its own habit toward another. Yet we see these things always being altered in quantity, quality, place, and time; and these alterations clearly accompany the lunar motions.

<summary>This page discusses how winds and coastal geography create irregularities in the tides, while beginning a defense of the theory that the moon, rather than internal "boiling" of the water, is the primary cause of tidal movement.</summary>
<keywords>tides, lunar motion, winds, oceanography, cardinal directions, ebb and flow, natural philosophy</keywords>

--- Page 47 ---
<meta>This page continues the natural philosophical discussion of tidal theory, specifically addressing the lunar influence on the seas and the classification of different bodies of water based on their movement.</meta>

¶ In the second place, since waters flowing outward toward the extremities run into a larger space than that which contained them, it is certainly necessary. If, therefore, the sea flows out of itself into wider places, by what force is it then repelled back into a space that is insufficient for it? For it is not in the nature of things that something would willingly refuse to remain where it is.
¶ In the third place, since the nature of water always tends downward and toward the depths, why is it that in the "access" <note>original: "accessu"; referring to the incoming tide or flood tide</note> we see the waters not moving downward, but by a contrary motion being carried toward the other side and climbing to higher ground? Since this is not in the nature of water itself, it was necessary that there be some external cause for the thing; and no other cause is found besides the moon.
¶ The fourth point meets the reasoning of those who think that for the same reasons, the same motions should also pass into fresh waters. In this case, the difference between the sea and fresh water is sufficient: for the waters of the sea are dense and salty <note>original: "salebre"; likely a variant of "salubris" or "salax," here meaning briny/salty</note> while those of rivers and that kind of distribution are subtle and fresh. Since these things are so, it is not necessary that everything which happens to one must follow for the other.

-> Chapter Eight. <-

<image-desc>Large decorative woodcut initial letter 'C' containing a profile view of a human face within a wreath of vines and flowers.</image-desc>Since, therefore, there is such great lunar power <note>original: "lune virtus"</note> in the nature of the sea, it seems to follow that a distinction should be made between the seas themselves—those which follow the lunar potency and the others. For as is well known among philosophers, the moon is indeed ineffective in no depth of the sea; but its force and efficacy are sometimes more manifest and sometimes less, not indeed because of any impediment in the moon itself, but because of the less adapted condition of the sea. Therefore, the condition of all seas is threefold. For there are some seas in which there is neither access nor recess <note>high tide and low tide</note>; others in which these motions occur but are not apparent; and others in which they both occur and are apparent.
Of the first two kinds, then, there are three species. In the first kind of the first species are those waters which are of the original seal <note>original: "primevo consigni"; perhaps referring to primordial or landlocked waters</note>, not thickened by long time nor salty, and thus not productive of the winds that incite those motions; they have refused this office not through any impotence, such as lakes and marshes which, from some flood in a receptacle, grow in the likeness of a confused sea in the winter storm and decrease in the summer heat. Of the second species are the seas entering far from the circuit of the moon, so that the lunar force cannot reach them. Of the third species are those <insert>sacred</insert> waters whose bottom is soft and shifting, which, since it yields to the impulses of the waters, offers nothing to force them back into an "access," which frequently happens around promontories and islands.
In the second kind of the first species are the seas whose shores are either separated by such great width from one another or are so far removed from human habitation that, although they may ebb and flow, there is no one present to indicate those motions. Of the second species are the seas whose shores are distant in width and are not uninhabitable, but are so steep, hostile, and high, lacking a level plain, that although the rest of the waters move, they have no place to overflow. Of the third species are the waters flowing into others; for these "accesses" <note>tides</note> occur at the hours in which they flow in.
Of the third kind are the seas of the primordial flow, which are crossed with difficulty even with the expenditure of time, being near the lunar circle, whose bottom is deep, rough, and rocky, and whose shores are flat and habitable, such as the Indian, Persian, <unclear>Scintum</unclear>, and Gallic <note>French</note> seas, and others like them, which move not by themselves but by a certain office of the moon, as has been explained. It has been demonstrated, however...

<summary>This page argues that the moon is the primary external cause of tides, explaining why water moves upward against its nature during a flood tide. It also categorizes different types of seas and lakes based on whether their tidal movements are visible, non-existent, or hindered by geography.</summary>

--- Page 48 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of celestial influence on the terrestrial world, moving from the tides of the sea to the biological effects of the moon on living organisms.</meta>

The motions of the bodies of the lower world follow the paths and returns of the celestial bodies, drawn by certain secret bonds of nature, as has been shown above through examples among terrestrial bodies themselves. In this same manner, the Moon draws the seas—which are congruent to its own nature—at an equal distance by a certain secret kinship.

The Sun also possesses some power over the sea. For example, the Indian and Persian <note>original: "indicū 7 pficū"</note> seas are indeed one continuous body of water, yet they possess contrary properties following the solar circuit. For the Persian Gulf, from the beginning of Virgo until the head of Pisces, remains swollen with its own winds and storms, making it unnavigable for any ship. Conversely, from Pisces back to Virgo, it is calm and compliant. Indeed, its most violent heat rages at the beginning of Sagittarius, while it rests most powerfully at the first point of Gemini. The Indian Sea is the opposite: for as long as the Persian heat rages, the Indian Sea grows cold; similarly, at the first point of Gemini, the Indian Sea rages most, while at the beginning of Sagittarius, it is most mild. So great is their discrepancy that the nature of the Persian Sea is judged "melancholic," while the Indian is "choleric" <note>The author is applying the theory of the four humors to geographic bodies of water.</note>. The full extent of these seas has been emerged <insert>mapped</insert> by the most famous sailors within certain boundaries. For the Persian Gulf begins on the eastern side from the innermost islands of Terses and Argues, leading westward toward the lands of the Sabaeans and Arabs as far as the ports of Aditi; from there, passage is made to Egypt and Syria. The Indian Sea, however, begins on the eastern side from the furthest borders of India, flowing around the island of Prasane, and thus washing various provinces of India and Ethiopia, it extends as far as the eastern shores of the Persian Gulf. These seas, therefore, according to the different character of their shores, also feel different affections regarding the diversity of the solar circuit. Nevertheless, the Sun's power is felt in other seas as well, which suffices to have demonstrated for the time being.

### Chapter 9: On the leadership of the Moon over animals and plants.

<image-desc>Large ornamental woodcut initial 'H' containing a stylized landscape with a building or temple entrance, above which sits a crescent moon and a star.</image-desc> Thus far, we have followed the singular power of the Moon in the nature of the sea and waters as much as was necessary. Next, the leadership of the Moon must be followed in the various affections of the bodies of the lower world themselves. For the frequent ministry of lunar power is found not only in the movements of the elements, but even in the daily uses of things. There are indeed many kinds of things which, as long as the Moon increases and ascends over their locations, indulge more largely in growth; as long as it decreases and descends from their locations, they withdraw their growth. This is seen in animals, plants, and metals: for thus, while the Moon is waxing, the humors <note>The vital fluids of the body: blood, phlegm, yellow bile, and black bile.</note> abound in the bodies of animals; while it is waning, they are thinned, so that you will find the veins of the human body fuller in the former case and slacker in the latter, which appears more clearly in the sick. For those who fall ill in the first half of the lunar cycle find their nature stronger and more powerfully resistant to the disease; in the following half, being weaker, they succumb more easily. Even the daily affections of the sick are measured by the daily applications of the Moon to its original place—in trine, quartile, or opposition—and its returns; because of this privilege, these days are distinguished from others by a specific name <note>Likely referring to "critical days" in medical astrology.</note>. For sailors also, discerning these days, perceive the patterns of winds, clouds, and rains. Among these things is also the fact that whenever...

<summary>This page explains how the Sun and Moon influence the tides and temperatures of the Persian and Indian seas, and begins a chapter on the Moon's astrological influence over biological humors, health, and plant growth.</summary>
<keywords>astrology, tides, Persian Gulf, Indian Ocean, lunar phases, four humors, medical astrology, critical days, celestial influence</keywords>

--- Page 49 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of lunar influences on the natural world and introduces the fourth book of the treatise, focusing on the nature of the seven planets according to Ptolemy.</meta>

Men who go about at night under the light of the moon are affected by a certain sluggishness and rarity <note>The author suggests that moonlight "thins" or weakens the human constitution</note>. There is also the fact that the flesh of animals exposed to the moon at night changes somewhat in both smell and taste. Furthermore, in animals, that which is cold and moist—such as milk, the brain, and marrow—abounds more when the moon is waxing and is diminished when it is waning. Or otherwise, the white of an egg conceived and laid in the first half of the lunar cycle changes more abundantly; all these same things occur both day and night as the moon ascends and descends in different places through the quadrants of its circle. 

Certain kinds of fish also come out from their caves through various waters in the first half of the lunar cycle, but remain enclosed in the following half. Likewise, as the moon ascends, they are carried to the upper waters; as it descends, they sink to the depths. Or otherwise, worms and reptiles, as well as hawks, are sharper and more fervent in their hunting during the first half of the lunar cycle. Grafting also favors the waxing and ascending moon and its increases, accelerating the fruit, but less so when the moon is waning and descending. 

However, the moon dries out and scorches many sprouts, such as the *licium* <note>likely referring to a species of buckthorn or a specific medicinal plant</note>. How great its power is in sprouts, herbs, seeds, and roots is most proven by the common experiments of those devoted to their cultivation. Nor are metals entirely immune to lunar power; those who know them find them more worthy when the moon is waxing and less worthy when it is waning. There are many such duties of the moon's leadership in the things of the world, so many that it would be impossible to follow them all by numbering each one; anyone would labor in vain to do so.

### ¶ The nine chapters of the fourth book.

<image-desc>Large decorative woodcut initial 'P' depicting two figures in classical robes engaged in astronomical or philosophical observation. One figure points upwards towards the heavens while the other holds a curved instrument or bow.</image-desc>
First, on the nature of the seven stars <note>The "seven stars" refer to the seven classical planets: Moon, Mercury, Venus, Sun, Mars, Jupiter, and Saturn</note> according to Ptolemy. ¶ Second, on their nature and likewise their fortune and misfortune according to certain common astrologers. ¶ Third, on those who change the nature, fortune, and misfortune of the stars based on their colors. ¶ Fourth, on the discovery of their fortune and misfortune through physical investigation. ¶ Fifth, on the distinction between the fortunate and the unfortunate. ¶ Sixth, on the diversity of the condition of both kinds of stars in the transition of one to another. ¶ Seventh, on the change of the stars and the nature of their effects in the movements of the seasons. ¶ Eighth, on masculine and feminine stars. ¶ Ninth, on diurnal <note>daytime</note> and nocturnal <note>nighttime</note> stars.

### ¶ On the nature of the seven stars according to Ptolemy. Chapter 1.

<image-desc>Smaller decorative woodcut initial 'P' with ornate floral and foliate patterns.</image-desc>
After Alexander the Macedonian, the kings of Greece are said to have ruled Egypt for 275 years. Of these, ten succeeding in a row were all called by the single name of Ptolemy. One of them, born in Philadelphia <note>referring to a city in the Decapolis or Egypt, not the American city</note> and reigning in Egypt, wrote the book of astronomy called the *Almagest* <note>original: "Almagesti"</note> in the Greek Ionic language. To the same man, some also ascribe the four parts of the treatise on astrology <note>referring to the Tetrabiblos</note>, and many to each of the others...

<summary>This page details the moon's biological and physical influences on animals, plants, and metals before transitioning into Book Four, which examines the nature of the seven planets according to Ptolemy.</summary>
<keywords>lunar cycles, Ptolemy, Almagest, astrology, agriculture, animal husbandry, planetary nature, Alexander the Great, Egypt</keywords>

--- Page 50 ---
<meta>This page continues a critique of traditional Aristotelian or Ptolemaic planetary physics, specifically questioning how celestial bodies can possess elemental qualities like heat or moisture without being subject to decay.</meta>

...that it matters nothing to us to confirm it in such a way or otherwise: except that in my own book <note>The author refers to his own previous writings or a specific chapter</note> the natures of the stars differ: he <note>Likely referring to Aristotle or a specific commentator</note> has followed the causes of things with less accuracy. For, beginning with the Sun, he affirms it is hot based on experience, for in approaching it brings heat, and in receding it leaves cold behind. He claims the Moon is moist because, being near to the Earth, it is affected by the Earth's rising vapors. He says Saturn is cold and dry because it is far removed from both the heat of the Sun and the vapors of the Earth. Mars is hot and dry as the heat of fire confirms, since it seethes with rising heat due to its proximity to the Sun. Jupiter is tempered because it sits in the middle between Saturn and Mars. Venus is also hot and moist, the former due to its proximity to the Sun, the latter due to the vapors of the Earth reaching all the way to it. Mercury, however, is sometimes dry and sometimes moist, according as it now rises toward the Sun or now declines toward the circle of the Moon.

Some who have searched more deeply into the nature of things cannot pass over such an unforeseen conclusion from so great a man without wonder. For it is established from the books of celestial distances that the circle of the Moon, where it is closest to the Earth, is distant from the Earth's surface by 128,000 miles—or truly 94,000 miles—with each mile consisting of three thousand <term>cubits</term> <note>A cubit is an ancient unit of length based on the distance from the elbow to the tip of the middle finger</note>. However, the Philosopher <note>Standard medieval reference to Aristotle</note> measured that the vapors of the Earth are known to rise no more than 17 <term>stadia</term> <note>A stadium is a Greek unit of distance, roughly 185 meters</note> from the surface. A stadium is four hundred cubits, which makes [the vapor's height] two miles and one stadium. Since these things are so, what is this great "proximity" of the Moon and Earth that he speaks of here: that the nature of the Moon is infected by the vapors of the Earth? 

For if the Moon were capable of receiving the Earth's vapors, it would necessarily follow that it would suffer what bodies capable of receiving vapors suffer: namely, corruption and dissolution <note>The author argues that if the Moon were made of "earthly" stuff enough to absorb steam, it would rot or break apart like earthly objects</note> once infected by them. Furthermore, when he asserts that Mars, placed below the Sun, seethes with rising heat, he seems to think the Sun exerts the same force upon the upper world as it uses by its power upon the lower world. But the Sun brings the heat of fire to the lower world according to the virtue of fire, which is an element of the sublunary world. Thus, it would follow that the celestial bodies are also capable of receiving these elements. 

Why then does Mars not turn black from the continuous effect of heat? Why does Saturn not grow pale in incurable cold? Why is the former not eventually consumed by unceasing flame, or the latter destroyed by perpetual frost? For we see these results follow naturally in bodies capable of these forces. Thus, Venus draws heat neither from the vapors of the Earth nor from proximity to the Sun. Likewise, Saturn is not cold and dry because it is far from both, since proximity is not the cause of opposites. Similarly for Jupiter, if it were tempered by the cold of Saturn and the heat of Mars meeting from either side. If indeed equalities meeting from elsewhere constituted its nature, then from their approaches and withdrawals—from which these qualities are drawn—it would be necessary for one or the other to succumb; an accident which usually results in corruption and dissolution in things capable of these qualities. 

The same reasoning shows that Mercury is neither dry nor moist for the reasons he sets forth. For if the bodies of the stars contracted these qualities from elsewhere, it would be necessary for their substances to be capable of receiving those qualities within themselves. But these are elementary qualities: therefore, it would follow that the substances of the stars are composed of these elements, which was rejected at the beginning of this treatise. For celestial bodies are not composed of these elements, since they are of another certain simple essence, with a simple form and simple effect, and by their natural motion they provide leadership to these accidents <note>The author argues for the "Fifth Essence" or Aether, suggesting stars influence the elements without being made of them</note>.

<summary>The author critiques the Aristotelian view that planets derive their physical qualities (heat, moisture) from their proximity to the Sun or Earthly vapors. He uses mathematical distances to prove that Earth's vapors cannot reach the Moon and argues that if planets were made of earthly elements, they would decay or burn up.</summary>
<keywords>astronomy, planetary physics, Aristotle, celestial distances, elements, quintessence, atmospheric vapor, corruption and dissolution</keywords>

<meta>This page begins Chapter 2 of the treatise, transitioning from a general introduction to the specific physical and metaphysical qualities of the stars and elements. It establishes the foundational "humoral" theory used in medieval science.</meta>

### ¶ Concerning the nature of the stars, as well as their good fortune and misfortune. Chapter 2.

<image-desc>A large decorative historiated or floriated woodcut initial 'Q' begins the text, depicting floral patterns and foliage within a double-bordered square.</image-desc>

Since we have proposed to treat the nature of the stars—their fortunes and misfortunes—it is appropriate in the first place to set forth the somewhat unsuitable opinion held by certain men. Afterward, we shall show what the contrary view is, and finally, our own understanding will be made clear according to philosophical investigation. Here, therefore, since we must follow the unlearned <note>The author likely refers to "popular" or "common" views before providing a more rigorous academic critique.</note> whose opinions we are discussing, we shall follow a less subtle reasoning and common speech regarding the natures and properties of the four elements and their mixtures in bodies, in a way that accords with their understanding.

For they say that the entire ancient opinion of the wise clearly agrees on this understanding: namely, that the number of all things in the sublunary world <note>The region below the moon, i.e., the Earthly realm subject to change and decay.</note> is comprised under two categories. These are first the four elements, and then the works composed from their mixture. The elements are fire, water, air, and earth. The mixtures, however, are choler, blood, phlegm, and melancholy <note>These are the four "humors" or bodily fluids believed to govern health and temperament.</note>. Furthermore, a nature and property belongs to each element, but not color or flavor, which instead belong to things made of the elements. For the color seen in fire is not the fire itself, but that of the burning matter; its nature is heat and its property is burning. So also with air, whose nature is moisture and whose property is a generative temperament. Likewise, the color of water is not truly water, but belongs to the substance containing the water; its nature is cold and its property is the foundation of things. To the earth also, which appears to have various colors, these belong to its various vapors; its nature is dryness and its property is the sustaining of things.

It is no different regarding flavor, of which fire and air are clearly devoid. The different locations of earth and water provide different flavors, and this is the opinion of some, though not all. Indeed, they all agree in part and disagree in part; they agree on the natures and properties. However, some deny colors and flavors to all elements, while others grant them to some. Among these there is a further sub-division: some take away one thing among flavors and colors, others take away another—some from one element, some from several. To these elements, therefore, they grant qualities: asserting that water is white in color and sweet in flavor, and the earth is dark <note>original: "fustam," likely meaning dusky or tawny</note> in color and acid in flavor. Some seem to assert the flavor of the earth is sweet for the reason that unless it were sweet, it could not sprout seeds. While denying flavor to fire, they grant it a red color, the reason for which they derive from the way it shines with flames.

But just as they deny flavor, they also remove color from the air because it is capable of contrary natures—now white, now black, now of middle complexions. From this, he <note>The unnamed authority being cited.</note> distributed to each its own nature and property, as well as its color and flavor. Therefore, just as we perceive color by sight and flavor by taste, so we detect their natures—that is, the hot, the dry, the cold, and the moist—through color and flavor; but we detect the property through the effect when one thing touches another.

Thus, the color of **choler** is fiery, its flavor is bitter, its nature is hot and dry, and its power <note>term: "virtus" (virtue/power) refers to the inherent active quality or function of a substance.</note> is to scorch. The color of **blood** is red, its flavor is sweet, its nature is hot and moist, and its power is generative. The color of **phlegm** is white, its flavor is tasteless, its nature is cold and moist, and its power is nutritive. The color of **melancholy** is dark, its flavor is salty, its nature is cold and dry, and its power is retentive. These are the things spoken concerning the elements and their mixtures; let us now pass on to the bodies of the stars.

<summary>This page explains the medieval scientific view of the four elements (fire, air, water, earth) and the four humors, detailing how their physical properties like color and taste relate to their internal natures.</summary>
<keywords>four elements, humors, choler, blood, phlegm, melancholy, Aristotelian physics, astrology, properties of matter</keywords>

--- Page 52 ---
<meta>This page continues a discussion on the physical properties of celestial bodies, specifically addressing the Chaldean method of determining planetary natures through color observation.</meta>

...whose natures they derive from colors alone: not from tastes, which they were unable to perceive due to the impossibility of tasting them. Thus, the Chaldeans <note>The author refers to ancient Babylonian astronomers/astrologers as "Chaldeans," a common Renaissance term.</note> seem to have convinced themselves that they discovered the dry, cold, or moist natures of the stars according to the diversity of their colors. They believe that the natures of things follow their colors, asserting that knowledge of things reaches the intellect through a certain kinship—moving from things that are near and known to those that are far distant. Thus, proceeding from the elements and their mixtures to the stars, they judge the stars' natures by their colors. 

For they say that whatever star has the color of this or that element, or of this or that temperament <term>complexio: the balance of the four bodily humors or elemental qualities</term>, that star must possess that same nature. If a star's color is a mixture of different colors, it is likewise of a mixed nature. For example, since the dark, brownish color of earth and melancholy <note>original: "melancolice"</note> indicates a cold and dry nature, they judge that the same color in Saturn indicates the same nature, property, and power. Likewise, they judge the fiery natures of Mars and the Sun; in the case of the Sun, this is even confirmed by its visible effects. 

They claim the color of Venus is midway between white and bluish-gray <note>original: "glaucum"</note>. It does not represent any single simple color of a temperament, but just as it is a mixture of bile and phlegm, so its nature is a temperate middle between hot and cold—that is, it represents a warm and moist nature. Likewise, the whitish color of Jupiter demonstrates its temperate nature, a power favorable to generation. Because the Moon <note>original: "D_" (symbol for Luna)</note> is marked with a white color mixed with dark, its nature is understood as cold because of the dark and moist because of the white. 

Because we see Mercury as multicolored—appearing now green, now gray, or something different from these—they say it has a varied nature according to the reception of various colors. By this reasoning, having distinguished the natures of the stars, they find them to be either hot and moist or cold and moist. Since these qualities are suitable for birth and nourishment, they judged them to be "fortunate" <term>fortunate: planets considered "benefic" or lucky in astrology</term>. Those that are either hot and dry or cold and dry, they assigned to the "unfortunates" <term>infortunijs: "malefic" planets</term>, because these qualities are corruptive and immoderate. When a nature is varied or unknown, they distinguish it as prosperous when with the benevolent and adverse when with the harmful. By this reasoning, Saturn and Mars fell into the harmful category; Jupiter, Venus, and the Moon into the better category. Mercury is considered changeable. The Sun also, although fiery like Mars, is sometimes added to the fortunates and sometimes to the unfortunates, because it is the general source of heat and nourishment.

---

### ¶ Concerning those who change the nature, fortune, and misfortune of the stars based on their colors.
#### Chapter 3.

<image-desc>Square decorative woodcut initial 'H' featuring a central face surrounded by leaf-like ornamentation.</image-desc>Having explained these things, we must now apply what may refute these deceived opinions. They seem to be entirely corrupted by a fourfold reasoning. First, because the color of Saturn is different from both the earthy and the melancholic; it is rather leaden. Furthermore, if the color of Jupiter is tinged with bluish-gray, how can it be called white, since white ceases to be white when stained by any other color? [The same applies to] the star of Mars. Moreover, the color of Venus is clearly sub-whitish. Since no one doubts that the Sun is hotter than Mars, if color relates to nature, it would be necessary for the Sun to be redder than Mars. That this is otherwise is not unknown even to their own eyes. Nor should Mercury be assigned various natures for its various colors...

<summary>This page explains the traditional "Chaldean" system of assigning astrological qualities (hot, cold, moist, dry) to planets based on their colors, before beginning a skeptical rebuttal of these associations. It details why certain planets like Jupiter are considered "fortunate" while others like Saturn are "unfortunate" based on elemental theory.</summary>
<keywords>astrology, planetary colors, Chaldeans, four humors, Saturn, Jupiter, Mars, Venus, Mercury, Sun, Moon, benefic, malefic</keywords>

--- Page 53 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's argument against simplistic color-based astrology and begins a new chapter on the physical investigation of "fortunate" and "unfortunate" stars.</meta>

...of which they claim the causes are not the stars' own natures, but rather our own sight. Since the moon does not appear the same near the horizon, the various vapors of the earth coming between our sight infect the moon with their own colors; finally, no one who enjoys healthy vision would judge it to be truly white. ¶ In the second place, since there is no comparison between different kinds of things, the bodies of the stars do not represent these colors for this reason: it is not necessary for their natures to be stained by these qualities, since even if that happens in the bodies of this world, they are not of the same kind—a difference which no learned person ignores. ¶ In the third place, we do not grasp the natures of either celestial or terrestrial bodies through colors as is commonly thought; we know many things of the same color yet of different natures. For example, look at snow and white hair <note>original: "cana"</note>; both are indeed equally white, yet one is cold and wet, while the other is hot and dry. Likewise, honey and aloes are of almost the same color, yet of contrary natures. There are many things of this sort, but conversely, there are some whose nature is known by their effect rather than by their color. ¶ In the fourth place, since he judges those stars to be "fortunate" which reach the elements from which something is produced, and since the generation of all things occurs from all of them, and they assert that each star corresponds to each element, it follows that all stars must be fortunate.

### ¶ On the discovery of the fortune and misfortune of the stars through physical investigation. Chapter 3.

<image-desc>Decorative woodcut initial N featuring a floral or architectural motif.</image-desc>

Now the order of our work requires us to suggest by what <gloss>distinction physical investigation separates the fortunate and the unfortunate stars.</gloss> The purer understanding of all philosophy, as it first looked upon the nature of every thing in this world as being between the beneficial and the harmful, immediately distributed them into the order of "fortune" and "misfortune"; it decreed that each should be called by the general name of its order. Wherefore, among all kinds of things, whatever exists that is suitable for the birth and temperament <note>The "temperament" refers to the balance of the four humors or elements in a person.</note> of the elements, for the composition of bodies, for the span of life, for health, for comforts, and for the dignities of both soul and body, ought by right to be set apart by the name of "better fortune." However, whatever tended toward the detriment, corruption, and destruction of all these things, he concluded—not without merit—should fall into the contrary category. Having understood these things, it must be remembered what we believe was explained from the beginning of this treatise: that the motions and qualities of the stars [produce] the effects of those motions through this world, which is bound to them by a certain natal affection. Now, however, we must add that the seven stars <note>The "seven stars" refer to the classical planets: Sun, Moon, Mercury, Venus, Mars, Jupiter, and Saturn.</note> carry out the principal ministry of every effect in this world, because they run their courses individually and in various ways. Indeed, many and dissimilar circles of each star are the causes of this kind of discourse. For although any of the wandering stars <note>term: "wandering stars" (planetae). Unlike the "fixed" stars, planets appear to move against the background of the constellations.</note> moves by its own natural motion, never slowing down nor accelerating but always carried equally in the middle, it is nevertheless necessary for it to be held back at times and moved in the circle of retrogradation <note>term: "retrogradation." The optical illusion where a planet appears to move backward in the sky.</note>, in the eccentric circle, and in the zodiac <note>original: "signifero"</note>, which measures the manner, quantity, and divisions of both. For these reasons, it is necessary that they be perpetually altered both in their motions—indeed, even in their various inequalities—and in the various differences of their colors. Reason holds that we should follow these inequalities of natures and properties of the stars within their variety. Thus, philosophy determines the natures of the stars, which it describes as spherical bodies encompassing the world perpetually in a circular motion. Their properties, however, [are derived] from the effects of these motions through this world...

<summary>The author concludes his critique of using color to judge planetary nature, arguing that physical effects are more reliable. He begins Chapter 3, defining "fortunate" and "unfortunate" stars based on whether their physical influence supports or destroys life and health.</summary>
<keywords>astrology, physics, fortune, misfortune, planetary motion, retrogradation, elements, temperament, seven planets</keywords>

--- Page 54 ---
<meta>This page continues a philosophical and astrological discussion regarding the influence of celestial bodies on the generation and corruption of earthly things, specifically defining the nature of "fortune" and "misfortune" in a cosmological context.</meta>

When they see that one part of the nature of things is suited for birth <note>original: "geniture"</note> and nourishment, while another part inclines toward the corruption and destruction of things, the laws of nature decreed that one part should be noted by the name "fortunate" and the other "unfortunate." For these categories never conceive either type of fortune within their own essence, but they always represent both within us. Hence, it was necessary to distinguish between nature <note>the creative principle</note> and the "natured" <note>the physical result or created thing</note>, the latter of which they understood through the cause—namely, the effect. For whatever is born would never be brought forth unless it existed in nature; not in actual deed, but in potential, until specific origins <note>or "seeds"</note> coming together are produced in act, with the celestial circuit from above providing the stimulus.

Therefore, since this mode of movement was subject to such circumstances in the effect of these things, it was necessary for it to fall into one of the two parts of fortune. For a human and a donkey are indifferent in the "nature of animals"—neither is more or less an animal than the other—nor is the body of a human different from the body of a donkey in the "matter of things." But when the specific origins were added, immediately one came forth as a biped with its face turned upward toward the stars <note>original: "sydra"</note>, destined for reason and intellect; the other a quadruped, prone and content with irrational sense. Then, the singular properties of individuals are summoned within the species to finally shape the individual bodies of the world. To some of these, a stable life, a fitting habit, and dignity of form are granted, along with similar gifts. To others, however, the opposite happens. It was a logical consequence, then, that the part of the stimulating movements that brings life should be judged fortunate, and the part that is harmful should be judged noxious.

We imitate all these qualities of celestial power in three ways: First, according to the movement of each individual star. Second, according to the effect of one star upon another. Third, according to the elementary reception of these acts. For whatever is born from those and these responds to the act of the former by design, and to the mode of the latter's passion by its own generation. Therefore, when a fit and perfect generation comes forth from such a union, we conceive that the former acted suitably and the latter consented in a friendly manner; and we understand that to be the "fortunate part." "Misfortunes," however, are those things which nature suffers—abhorring them and enduring them against her will—whether she suffers miscarriages of happy beginnings or breaks out into ill-fated births. There is, therefore, a threefold offspring of this kind of birth: First, in the division of species. Second, of individuals. Third, in their own properties and accidents.

### ¶ On the distinction between the fortunate and the misfortunes. Chapter 5.

<image-desc>A large decorative initial 'Q' containing a five-petaled rose within a circular border, with foliate ornamentation in the four corners of the square block.</image-desc>

**Since** it has been demonstrated that among the stars some are fortunate and others are misfortunes, we must now discern which among them are the fortunes. Every composition of things proceeds from a certain consent of the elements, which the tempering of the seasons <note>original: "temporum temperies"</note> administers, following the preceding movements of certain stars. Therefore, whatever star leads by its own motion toward this kind of tempering and toward the generation of things and life is called "fortunate" for this world as it is. But that which declines toward the departure of nature, the impediment of generation, and corruption, is rightly called a "misfortune." By this reasoning, the ancient image finally distinguishes among the number of stars the fortunate, the misfortunes, the hot, the cold, the dry, the moist, the masculine, the feminine, the diurnal, the nocturnal, and others of that kind. For these stars are something of this sort in themselves by which they belong to the world, but their effects are of this kind among [us]...

<summary>This page explains the astrological and philosophical origins of "good" and "bad" fortune, attributing them to the harmonious or discordant movements of stars and elements during the process of birth and creation. It establishes that "fortunate" stars promote life and reason, while "unfortunate" stars lead to corruption and irrationality.</summary>
<keywords>astrology, fortune, misfortune, generation and corruption, celestial influence, elements, nature vs. natured, reason, species, planetary qualities</keywords>

--- Page 55 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of planetary influences on the seasons and the generation of life, specifically focusing on how individual planets act when they hold the "lordship" or primary influence over a year.</meta>

It is certain for us. Specifically, whenever **Saturn** <note>original: ♄</note> happens to obtain the leadership of the year's principality without the influence of **Mars** <note>original: ♂</note> or others, it intensifies the winter cold throughout all northern regions; it does so to such an extent that it kills many animals and hinders most buds with a dry cold, especially when it is ascending in its circle from the center. In southern places, however, it cools the burning air of the **Sun** <note>original: ☉</note> to such a degree that this temperament adds not a little to the life of animals, to health, to strength, and to the fertility of buds. Similarly, **Mars** obtaining the leadership of the year without the influence of **Saturn** or others mitigates the winter for northerners, but intensifies the summer for southerners. Meanwhile, it reaches a point where it benefits the former slightly with a tempering of the cold, but greatly affects the latter in this intemperate heat.

As was said above, the solar movements through the circle—the comings and goings—follow the seasons of the year. Hence, the participation of the stars with the **Sun** is the reason why **Saturn**, stirring up northern conditions in certain signs, increases winter cold and diminishes summer heat. And conversely, in certain friendly signs, it indulges the south so that it does not harm its own part and benefits the dryness somewhat. **Jupiter** <note>original: ♃</note>, however, mixed with the **Sun** through the seasons of the year and unhindered by others, brings the elements of the world from heat and moisture into a total equal generation of things, otherwise carrying out the principal counsel of the year's leadership. **Venus** <note>original: ♀</note> also, mixed with the **Sun** and unhindered by others in her seasons—especially in winter or spring—relieves the year with an equal moistening of the seasons, such as the dryness of summer and autumn, provided no other planet is overpowering in the lordship of the year. **Mercury** <note>original: ☿</note> also, mixed with the **Sun** and not meanwhile influenced by others, agitates that whole time with various qualities and diverse winds <note>original: venerisq; - likely a scribal error for ventisq; (winds)</note>, provided no other is ruling the year.

The **Moon** <note>original: ☽</note> truly is hot and moist in the first quarter of her lunation; hot and dry in the second; cold and dry in the third; and cold and moist in the fourth. For in each lunation, traveling through the whole circle, she imitates the divisions of the solar year. When she is set over the lordship of the year, or is mixed with the **Sun** through the seasons, and is unhindered by others, she ordains the quarters of the year according to the fourfold qualities of the lunation. It has seemed to some, however, that the **Moon** is hot and moist from conjunction to opposition, and then cold and moist; they likewise split the year of lunar lordship through the middle in the same way.

Therefore, since **Saturn** and **Mars** exuberate intemperately in both heat and cold—the surpassing abundance of which qualities is the cause of the corruption and death of things—they are not unjustly judged as <term>malefics</term> <note>original: infortunia; planets traditionally considered to have a negative or challenging influence</note>. Because **Saturn** is cold and dry, and this is a greater misfortune for generative qualities, it is the "Greater Malefic." Although a tempering may sometimes come from them in certain parts, this is by no means by their own virtue, but by the approaching or receding of the opposing **Sun**.

The **Sun**, however, through its motion and virtue, consists in the tempering of the elements, the composition of things, and the general sustenance of life. For these three reasons, it is recorded as being <term>fortunate</term> <note>original: fortunatus; a "benefic" or life-giving planet</note>. The **Moon** also, traveling through the zodiac with each lunation, renews the fourfold annual virtues of the **Sun** in the tempering of nature, in the effects on things, and in the powers of the sea's own change. She herself is also not undeservedly called fortunate, except that she is recognized as a greater fortune according to how much stronger and brighter she is. The virtue of **Jupiter**, moreover, is spent in the moderation of the seasons' temperament and is suitable for births. The property of **Venus** likewise is consumed in the tempering of the season and in healthy moistening; and these two are on the side of fortune...

☿ <note>Mercury</note> also has an effect that exists in a light change of times: so that it does not change from a temperate state at all: and it is itself grouped with the fortunate ones in its own nature. Except that, due to the great variety of its movements between direct and retrograde, being continually pulled slower or faster, it is found to be so capable of variety that it easily mixes with any others, yielding its own influence to theirs unless it is particularly powerful. Whence it happens that when mixed with the fortunate it is benevolent, and with the unfortunate it is harmful: with masculine stars it is masculine: with feminine, feminine: with diurnal, diurnal: with nocturnal, nocturnal; it finds its nature approaching whichever star it is with: which, when it lacks respect to all others and is not hindered in itself, is found to be fortunate: except insofar as the nature of the sign in which it resides either adds to or subtracts from either side. 

When, therefore, we wish to discern between these three fortunes <note>the benefic planets: Jupiter, Venus, and Mercury</note>, since we find ♃ <note>Jupiter</note> and ♀ <note>Venus</note> are lower: and we see ♀ is elongated from the ☉ <note>Sun</note> by 47 degrees, but ☿ by 27 degrees: and thus ☿ is "burned" <term>combust: a planet too close to the sun loses its power</term> more than ♀. However, ♃, being superior, is left at 180 degrees from the ☉; among these three fortunes, ♃ is attributed the greatest fortune because it stands out as the greatest; ♀ the middle or lesser; and ♿ the lowest or least. 

Thus, with both kinds distinguished: ♄ <note>Saturn</note> indeed exists as the greater misfortune; ♂ <note>Mars</note> as the lesser. In the most excellent part of the world, the ☉ is the primary fortune: after this the ☽ <note>Moon</note>: then ♃, ♀, ☿. In this manner, with the stars distinguished by either fortune, although fortune is attributed to these and the contrary to those, much diversity is nevertheless found in both kinds: so that ♂ and ♄, although they exceed in heat and cold, sometimes produce a temperate state for certain things; and for them, fortune is made out of misfortune. 

So, conversely, the fortunate stars frequently—according as they ascend or descend in their circles, stand still, move retrograde, or fall into combustion, fall, or destruction—take the turn of misfortune. Because, therefore, what appears concerning the fortune and misfortune of the stars is not indeed from their nature but from their property <note>distinguishing between "nature" (essential essence) and "property" (accidental or situational power)</note>. For if it were from nature, the effect of the ☉ would always follow; yet misfortune in its own state is always misfortune. The fortunate also are blessed in their own condition, although, as has been said, much variation of these accidental affections occurs. 

For example: the nature of fire is hot and dry: its property is burning; if this were of its nature, it would be necessary for everything hot and dry to burn. Consequently, however, there are various other effects of its property: for in one and the same hour, fire warms, dries, binds, strengthens the ground, and moistens: either in different bodies or even in the same body, either simultaneously or continuously, which are different from burning. Thus, therefore, both form and misfortune are not [essential] to some of the stars, but are a certain property and virtue: and so, the virtue of the kind being diverse, many other things frequently happen. 

This property of the stars is twofold: one indeed of true and immutable leadership, not necessary for the astrologer, such as the effect of the fortunate: fortune being the contrary of misfortune. And that is in the division of species under genera, and individuals under their species, the quality of their composition. For although this may have variation in the conceived seed or sowing or grafting: yet this variation is not sufficient to transmute the species itself, so that from the seed of a man something other than a man is made: or of a horse, something other than a horse: and so of the rest, both living things and seeds or metals. Indeed, nothing changes its species by sidereal leadership: but in them and around them, it changes quality, quantity, sta—

<meta>This page continues the discussion of astrological principles, specifically focusing on how the inherent nature of planets (benefic or malefic) is modified by their position and relationship to the zodiac.</meta>

...state: disposition: of all affections and accidents <note>In this context, "accidents" refers to external events or non-essential qualities happening to a person.</note> both internal and external: every variation follows the guidance of the stars. ¶ Another property of the astrological art concerns the various stars, each of which, according to its different disposition and state, leads to the various accidents of individual people: such as effects: the corruption of the body: status: quantity: quality: physical condition: form: color: the disposition of the mind: the senses: convenience: external occurrences of chance: and things similar to these. For in these matters it sometimes happens: that the fortunate stars <note>The "benefics," usually Jupiter and Venus.</note> cause harm: and the unfortunate stars <note>The "malefics," usually Saturn and Mars.</note> offer favor: according to the manner of their state and affections through the various circles and places of the signs, being either suited or unsuited to the accidents of things. For example, the property of Saturn <note>original: "♄"</note> is misfortune: yet when he is above the earth by day, in the east, and moving directly, being safe both in himself and in his place, he yields to the power of a fortunate star. In the same way, a fortunate star, conversely, is corrupted by disadvantages both of itself and of its locations. Therefore, the guidance of the stars is found to be twofold, between that which is inherent and that which is accidental: yet each is firm in its own kind at its own time: of which there is a five-part division.

¶ In the first place, the effects of good or evil belong properly to the power of the star at individual times, as in the division of the species of individuals: whence one is born better and more worthy than another.

¶ Second, at the same time, there are different effects in different things; for instance, when Saturn is the lord of the year, he brings intolerable cold to some parts of the earth: and a mild temperature to others.

¶ Third, there are different effects in different times, as in the northern parts of the world through the northern signs: and in the southern parts through the southern signs.

¶ Fourth, when a fortunate star takes the turn of misfortune, or an unfortunate star takes the turn of fortune.

¶ Fifth, how much the different stars add to the diversity of the motion of things. This distinction of both types of stars has been grasped by philosophical investigation: these things having been distinguished, the diversity of the dispositions of both kinds of stars must now be investigated.

¶ Although, as has been said, some are fortunate and others are unfortunate, a double cause is found for the transformation of either kind into the property of the other. One is in the disposition and affection of the star itself. The other is what the quality of the place adds: nevertheless, some are more prone to misfortune: others more inclined to fortune.

¶ Therefore, there is a proper disposition and affection for each, such as being hot, dry, cold, moist, diurnal, nocturnal, eastern, or western: among these are also the manner of walking <note>The speed or direction of a planet's movement.</note> and the rest of those things which are in them. But the quality of the places includes the house, lordship, triplicity <term>trigonus: a group of three zodiac signs belonging to the same element (Fire, Earth, Air, or Water)</term>, term, and the other dignities or their opposites. Misfortune occurs, therefore, if ever a blessed star from either kind takes the turn of a fortunate one: but when deserted by the better part of both, it is left harmful by its own power and more corrupted: conversely, fortunate stars condemned by both kinds perform the office of misfortunes: consequently, they are more powerful in their free effects of benevolence: just as Saturn, being the lord of the triplicity in the natural east and holding an angle <note>A "cardinal" or "angular" house, positions of great strength in a chart.</note>, being safe in himself and strong in his place, is not inconvenient to the nourishment of an infant and to life; but when harmed in both or deserted by the better part, he denies nourishment. Likewise, as the leader of gain and possessions, as has been said, he is ready for ruin and loss, or if safe and strong, he is favorable. Jupiter <note>original: "♃"</note> also, as the leader of nourishment, as explained, if safe, is healthy for nourishment and life; if corrupted, the opposite. Likewise, as the leader of gain, he gathers an abundance of health: but if corrupted, he suffers loss.

It outlines five ways that planetary influences vary, showing that even a "malefic" planet can become beneficial under the right astronomical conditions.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of planetary natures and their influence on the world, specifically focusing on how their effects change based on the time of day (diurnal vs. nocturnal) and their position in the zodiac.</meta>

Thus, since both types of stars <note>referring to the "benefic" and "malefic" planets</note> are found to be sometimes friendly and sometimes hostile to the affairs of the world, it follows that we should explain the nature of all these changing conditions. Let us say, therefore, that the nature of the day is temperately warm, while that of the night is cold and moist. Likewise, a star that is oriental <note>rising before the sun</note> is warm and moist, while one that is occidental <note>setting after the sun</note> is cold and dry—except for the **Moon** <term>$\text{\leftmoon}$</term> <note>original: "Luna"</note>, whose qualities differ between her rising and setting.

<image-desc>Woodcut illustration of the planet Saturn. A bearded man sits on a throne under a canopy, holding a large scythe. To his left is a star, and to his right is the astrological symbol for Saturn (♄). Beneath the throne are two circular medallions representing the zodiac signs Capricorn and Aquarius.</image-desc>

¶ Therefore, although **Saturn** <term>$\hbar$</term> <note>original: "Saturnus"</note> excels in coldness, in certain places he is softened by a temperate climate and becomes, as it were, fortunate for them. Thus, in the diurnal signs, being oriental and in some position of his own dignity, he bestows as much benefit as can be gathered from these conditions. Likewise, at night in the nocturnal signs, being occidental and in his fall or detriment, he is a complete misfortune; however much he loses of these disadvantages, that much is added to his fortune. Here, therefore, although he alternates between both sides of fortune, he is nevertheless much more prone to misfortune. **Mars** <term>$\text{\mars}$</term> similarly, because he abounds in heat, becomes a fortunate influence for the safety of things when he is in nocturnal signs at night (which are colder and moister) and is occidental in some dignity of his own. When affected by contrary conditions, he is harmful in proportion; and he, too, is more inclined toward misfortune.

<image-desc>Woodcut illustration of the planet Jupiter. A kingly figure wearing a crown and holding a scepter sits on a throne. To his left is a star, and to his right is the astrological symbol for Jupiter (♃). Beneath him are two circular medallions representing the zodiac signs Sagittarius and Pisces.</image-desc>

¶ **Jupiter** <term>$\text{\jupiter}$</term> also, as one whose nature is temperately warm, rendering him fortunate, adapts to the nature of the day. During the day, in diurnal signs and being oriental in his dignities, he is a full fortune; the more free and blessed he is, the more beneficial he becomes. At night, in nocturnal signs and being occidental in an adverse place, he suffers a turn toward misfortune—more so if he holds a part of the circle in an adverse place with these disadvantages, such as the eighth, sixth, or twelfth houses <note>These houses were traditionally considered "malefic" or unlucky in astrology</note>. However, because this star is more inclined toward fortune, he is slow to agree to misfortune and does so to a lesser degree.

<image-desc>Woodcut illustration of the Sun (Sol). A crowned king holding a scepter and a globus cruciger sits on a throne. To his left is a radiant sun with a face, and to his right is the astrological symbol for the Sun (☉). Beneath him is a circular medallion containing a lion, representing the zodiac sign Leo.</image-desc>

¶ The **Sun** <term>$\odot$</term> <note>original: "Sol"</note>, however, exists as the universal father of the generation of all things. He himself is the fortune of the world; yet sometimes through heat and sometimes through hostile cold, he approaches misfortune to such an extent that he exists as the cause of death and destruction for both living creatures and plants in many places. He becomes fortunate, therefore, by the trine <note>original: "trigono", an aspect of 120 degrees</note> and the sextile <note>original: "hexagono", an aspect of 60 degrees</note> aspects. Misfortune, however, comes through conjunction and opposition, or the square <note>original: "tetragono", an aspect of 90 degrees</note> aspect between both types of stars. For a conjunction with the **Sun** occurs in the places through which he passes in his circle. Opposition occurs in those places where he is furthest away. The square occurs in places that happen at specific parts of the year.

# On the Fortunes of the Planets

leaves it far behind. But the middle <note>referring to the aspect or relationship</note> of the **trine** <note>trigonus: an aspect of 120 degrees, considered very harmonious</note> and **sextile** <note>exagonus: an aspect of 60 degrees, considered favorable</note>, since it is of the diurnal signs and is proven to be especially blessed in any of its dignities <note>term: dignity; the strength a planet gains by being in a specific sign of the zodiac</note>, is the opposite of the contrary: and the fortune is greater than the misfortune.

->[Image: The planetary deities Jupiter and Venus. Jupiter sits on a chariot with wheels showing Sagittarius and Pisces. Taurus and Libra are on the throne. Symbols: ♃ and ♀]<-

¶ But **Jupiter** (♃) and **Venus** (♀), although they provide a tempered moisture and render things balanced, do not accommodate the night; yet at night in nocturnal signs and in their own dignities, they are a manifest fortune. To the extent that these conditions are lacking, their fortune is diminished, so that when affected by contraries, they may even fall into the part of misfortune; and more so in adverse places of the circle, where their readiness is led toward pleasures and offspring for fortune rather than toward misfortunes.

->[Image: The planetary deity Mercury. He wears a winged hat and boots, holds a caduceus, and rides a chariot with wheels showing Gemini and Virgo. Symbol: ☿]<-

¶ **Mercury** (☿), however, as he is capable of diversity, is easily turned toward either side. The **Moon** (☽) also, as she is a fortunate and nocturnal light by her change: at night in nocturnal signs and in some dignity of her own, she is manifestly of fortune; in the contrary, the opposite. And **Mercury** is more agreeable to these fortunes. These habits of the stars, therefore, and the accidents of their locations between either fortune, increase, change, or diminish; to these is added how much the mixture of the stars contributes, of which some are more capable and others less. ¶ For **Saturn** (♄), as he is of a gross nature and slow motion, brings something of good or evil; but in the beginnings of things at birth, he confirms a matter forever: so that although others may mix with him later, he changes little. **Jupiter** (♃) also, since he is slow in motion, is not easily moved from his primordial leadership when he is strong; thereafter he is moved by some mixture. **Mars** (♂), however, is not so rigid; as he is swifter, so his powers are more easily admitted. But the **Sun** (☉), as he carries the leadership of the alterations of the seasons, by which the mixtures of the stars are added to those things to be altered, transfers their powers to the lower world when they are received. **Venus** (♀), as she is swifter, is either more passive or proceeds much more easily with whatever things are mixed. For **Mercury** (☿), as he is common, bends toward any part. Finally, the **Moon** (☽), as she carries the turn of the daily use of things, required the greatest work of diversity; and therefore, as she more quickly receives the aspects and conjunctions of all, so she is more easily infected by the affects of all. Therefore, although misfortunes <note>referring to the "malefic" planets Saturn and Mars</note> may sometimes take the place of the benevolent ones, they are not however called fortunate. For no one can obtain this "fortune of misfortune" without being immune to it or without great labor, nor hold it without much vexation of mind, so that for the most part their fortune becomes an occasion for misfortune. Thus, although the fortunate ones <note>referring to the "benefic" planets Jupiter and Venus</note> may sometimes cause harm, they are not called misfortunes. For this misfortune of theirs is either so grave or has the appearance of something better to such an extent that a desired end usually follows it. The **Head of the Dragon** <note>term: Draconis caput; the North Node of the Moon, where it crosses the ecliptic moving north</note>, since the Moon ascends from it, is added to the fortunate things; the **Tail** <note>term: Draconis cauda; the South Node of the Moon</note>, from which she descends, to the misfortunes; yet both are mixed for various reasons, which we shall follow as we do with the other following "dragons."

<summary>This page explains how the astrological influence of planets like Jupiter, Venus, and Mercury changes based on their position, the time of day, and their "mixture" with other celestial bodies. It introduces the concept of the Lunar Nodes (the Dragon's Head and Tail) as factors of fortune and misfortune.</summary>

# Chapter 6.

## ¶ Concerning the diversity of the condition of both types of stars and their transition.

<note>The "two types of stars" refers to the planets, which were historically called "wandering stars."</note>

<image-desc>An ornate woodcut illustration of the planet Saturn personified as an old, bearded man sitting in a horse-drawn chariot. He holds a scythe in his left hand and a vessel in his right. The wheels of the chariot contain the zodiac signs for Capricorn and Aquarius. A star and the astrological symbol for Saturn (♄) are visible in the upper part of the image.</image-desc>

Now, therefore, it is appropriate to treat the natures of the stars and their changes. For it must be known that the bodies of the stars are by no means hot, cold, or moist in themselves; rather, their nature is assigned according to what appears through their influence. ¶ It is established, therefore, that the nature of individual stars consists of twin elements: one of which is stable and unmoved, except insofar as its power varies between increases and decreases due to different circumstances. The other, however, is changeable through various actions. For instance, Saturn—as far as has been discovered from its influence—is immoderately cold, and immoderate cold is indeed dry. ¶ Thus, we say the nature of Saturn is cold and dry. In this nature, the cold acts as the active, immobile element, except for how much it is occasionally increased or diminished. Dryness, however, is changed again; for as it is a passive element, it varies so easily that it is often transformed into another quality.

<image-desc>An ornate woodcut illustration of the planet Mars personified as a warrior in full plate armor with a plumed helmet, sitting in a chariot pulled by horses. He holds a sword in his right hand and a shield in his left. The wheels of the chariot contain the zodiac signs for Aries and Scorpio. A sun-like star and the astrological symbol for Mars (♂) are visible in the upper part of the image.</image-desc>

¶ For whenever Saturn ascends from the middle of the circle of its apsis <note>original: "absidis"; the highest or lowest point in an orbit</note>, it flourishes in cold dryness, and thus in the quadrant signs of the circle that are cold and dry, it is firm in both its natures since all things agree. For in hot and dry signs, the cold is indeed diminished, but the dryness is assisted. However, when descending (as we said) and in moist signs, the dryness is diminished. If it should be simultaneously in a moist quadrant, in the term <note>a specific subdivision of a zodiac sign</note> of a moist star, and at the same time away from the sun on a moist horizon—if, I say, all these things should coincide at once—it is necessary for the dryness to fail to such an extent that, with moisture succeeding it, the nature of Saturn is found to be cold and moist. It flourishes, therefore, in cold moisture as long as it descends from the middle of its apsis. If those things mentioned likewise coincide in moisture, it prevails; but when it is at the opposite of the apsis, its cold is weakened and the moisture is diminished. If it should then be in a hot, dry quadrant and in the term of a hot, dry star, at that very hour, the moisture being consumed, the dryness is recovered so that its nature is left cold and dry.

¶ Similarly, since Mars is found by its effect to be immoderately hot—and indeed immoderate heat is nothing if not dry—its nature is judged to be hot and dry. In this nature, the heat acts as the active element and does not vary in its movement except between increase and loss. Dryness, however, is occasionally changed just as it is in Saturn; for just as hot places diminish the cold of Saturn, so do cold places diminish the heat of Mars. Dry places assist the dryness of both, while moist places diminish it and...

<summary>This page explains the astrological theory that planets are not inherently hot or cold, but exert these qualities based on their position in the zodiac and their orbital apsis. It details how Saturn (typically cold/dry) and Mars (typically hot/dry) can have their secondary qualities altered by environmental astrological factors.</summary>
