they change. ¶ The nature of Jupiter <note>original: "Iouis"</note>, however, being of a temperate heat, is aided by locations of a similar nature; in contrary locations, it varies between each boundary of comparison, yet the heat remains unchanged. ¶ The Sun <note>original: "Sol"</note> is also naturally hot and dry; while it ascends in its circle, heat and dryness prevail, but as long as it descends, heat and moisture prevail, so that when it approaches moist places, it adds to the moisture. And so it is in the opposite part: for concerning hot and cold things, one favors heat and the other favors dryness. ¶ Regarding the nature of Venus <note>original: "Veneris"</note>, which is hot and moist, the same things follow as were said concerning the nature of Jupiter.

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration depicting the planet Mercury as a planetary deity. He is seated on a throne inside a chariot, wearing a winged petasos hat and holding a caduceus. To his right is the astrological symbol for Mercury. The chariot's wheels contain symbols associated with the zodiac signs Gemini (two figures) and Virgo (a winged figure holding a branch). In the upper left corner, a radiant sun with a human face is visible.</image-desc>

¶ Truly, the nature of Mercury <note>original: "Mercurij"</note>, as long as it ascends in its circle, is a mixture of a little dryness and cold; when descending, it is moist with a moderate coldness. Thus, being capable of all four qualities, it is easily changed by any mixtures. ¶ The Moon <note>original: "Luna"</note>, however, as she is in her circuit, renews the four seasons of the solar circuit by a certain change. In the first quadrant of the lunation, she is of a spring-like nature: if meanwhile she ascends in her eccentric circle, heat and moisture prevail; if she descends, the reverse occurs. In the second quadrant, when she ascends, dryness restrains the heat of a summery nature. When she descends, she renders it temperate. In the third, as she ascends, dryness prevails over cold; as she descends, cold prevails over dryness. Finally, in the fourth, ascending brings cold, while descending prefers moisture. ¶ To these are added the qualities of the locations, just as was said of the others. Furthermore, the nature of the three superior stars <note>Saturn, Jupiter, and Mars</note>, from the time they are oriental <note>rising before the sun</note> until their first station, is dry; and until they enter combustion <note>getting too close to the sun's heat</note>, they are cold. Of the two inferior stars <note>Venus and Mercury</note>, from the time they are oriental by retrograde motion until they move direct, their nature is moist. From then until their conjunction with the sun, they are hot. Once they are occidental <note>setting after the sun</note> until they begin to retrograde, they are dry. From there until the conjunction of the sun, they are cold. The nature of the Head <note>the North Node of the Moon</note> is hot. The nature of the Tail <note>the South Node of the Moon</note> is cold.

### ¶ Concerning the change of the stars and their nature and effect on the movement of the seasons. Chapter 8.

**It follows** that among the stars whose nature has been explained, a distinction of sex should also be made. For since whatever is brought forth in this world is first conceived by the union of sexes, then shaped in nature, and finally brought into the light by a mature birth—and since the bodies of the stars are the primordial causes of the generations of this world—it was fitting that a distinction of sex be made among the stars themselves. Since, in the property of sex, the male is indeed of a hot and dry nature and an active power, while the female is of a cold and moist nature and a passive power—the union of which (that is, the action of one with the passion of the other) mixes a temperament for the offspring—judgment has assigned that stars of a hot nature and active power are male, while those that are moist and of a passive power are female. Thus, therefore, the Sun, Mars, and Jupiter are male. Saturn...

<summary>This page details how the elemental qualities (heat, cold, moisture, dryness) of planets fluctuate based on their orbital positions and introduces the astrological classification of planets as male or female based on these qualities.</summary>
<keywords>astrology, planetary qualities, Mercury, Moon, planetary gender, active and passive powers, celestial mechanics</keywords>

--- Page 62 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of planetary qualities, specifically focusing on the gender of the planets and the distinction between diurnal (daytime) and nocturnal (nighttime) influences. It concludes with a table of contents for the fifth book.</meta>

Since [Saturn] is cold and dry—and while cold is an active element, dryness belongs to the nature of heat—it has moved into the masculine category over time. Whatever is devoid of heat, nature leads toward the masculine as weak, of moderate virility, such as males who are chilled, idle, cowardly, sterile, and impotent in love. Regarding **Mercury**, the abundant dryness in him (which is related to the nature of heat) causes him to be numbered among the masculine; because such dryness, though separate from heat, renders the mind sharp, it leads toward the masculine through a keen intellect and high prudence, though with a neglect of and moderate vigor in love. Yet, because this dryness is a passive element, he easily alternates between both sexes. For this reason, **Venus** and the **Moon** are left as feminine. The Head <note>The North Node of the Moon, or Caput Draconis</note> also looks toward the masculine, while the Tail <note>The South Node of the Moon, or Cauda Draconis</note> looks toward the feminine. Although this distinction is natural in this way, an alternation of gender among the stars happens for various reasons, as has been said of their nature. For those in the East are fashioned as masculine, while those in the West are of feminine lightness; thus the quadrants of the circle from the rising to the peak, and from the setting to the bottom, are masculine, while the remaining parts take the turn of the feminine. To this is added how much the circles of the signs and houses contribute to each sex.

### ¶ Concerning the Diurnal and Nocturnal. Chapter 9.

<image-desc>A large woodcut historiated initial 'R' decorated with intricate leafy scrollwork and floral patterns within a square border.</image-desc>
It remains for us to distinguish between the diurnal <note>daytime</note> and nocturnal <note>nighttime</note> stars; for the fact that the nature of some is found more suited to the day and others to the night required this very distinction. Thus, **Saturn**, because his nature is tempered by the day (as has been said), is diurnal. ¶ **Jupiter** likewise, insofar as his nature is diurnal. ¶ **Mars**, however, whose nature is mitigated by the night, is nocturnal. ¶ The **Sun** also, as a diurnal light, is diurnal. ¶ **Venus**, insofar as her nature is nocturnal, is also nocturnal. Hence it is that when she is in the West—since that part is more inclined to the nocturnal and feminine nature—she is found to be fortunate. For in the East and by day above the earth and in masculine signs, she is diminished. ¶ **Mercury**, however, since he is hot by day (dryness being kindred to heat), is improperly under his own leadership. In the East he is diurnal, in the West nocturnal; he is stronger here than there, yet moving between both types when mixed with other stars. ¶ Finally, the **Moon**, as a nocturnal light, is nocturnal. ¶ The Head is also diurnal, the Tail nocturnal; and so it is with the heads and tails of the "dragon stars" <note>The lunar nodes</note>. Therefore, diurnal stars are indeed stronger in the East than nocturnal ones; conversely, although a star may be diurnal, it is stronger in the West if it is nocturnal <note>referring to the sect or condition of the planet</note>; for a direct star becomes western, and a retrograde star becomes eastern.

->¶ The 18 Chapters of the Fifth Book.<-

**First**, concerning the dignities <term>dignitatibus: the various strengths or honors a planet holds in a specific zodiac sign</term> of the stars. ¶ **Second**, concerning the houses <term>domicilijs: the zodiac signs ruled by specific planets</term> of the stars according to other astrologers. ¶ **Third**, concerning the houses of the stars according to **Ptolemy**. ¶ **Fourth**, according to **Hermes** following **Abidemon**. ¶ **Fifth**, concerning the rulership of the stars according to other astrologers. ¶ **Sixth**, concerning the rulership of the stars according to **Ptolemy**. ¶ **Seventh**, according to **Hermes** following **Abidemon**. ¶ **Eight**, concerning the methods and diversity of the terms <term>terminorum: specific subdivisions of zodiac signs ruled by different planets</term>. ¶ **Ninth**, concerning the Egyptian terms according to **Ptolemy**...

...of mine and of others. ¶ Tenth, on the <term>bounds</term><note>Latin: *terminis*. These are specific sub-divisions of a zodiac sign where a planet gains extra strength.</note> of the Indians. ¶ Eleventh, on the bounds of the Chaldeans. ¶ Twelfth, on the <term>decans</term><note>Ten-degree segments of a sign.</note> and their lords according to the Persians, Babylonians, and Egyptians. ¶ Thirteenth, on the decans and their lords according to the Indians. ¶ Fourteenth, on the ninth-parts of the signs according to the Indians. ¶ Fifteenth, on the twelfth-parts of the signs and the lords of each. ¶ Sixteenth, on masculine and feminine degrees. ¶ Seventeenth, on bright, dark, straight, and crooked degrees. ¶ Eighteenth, on the "pits" of the stars in the signs, as well as those adding to good fortune.

## ¶ On the Dignities of the Stars. Chapter 1.

The seven stars <note>The seven classical planets: Sun, Moon, Mercury, Venus, Mars, Jupiter, and Saturn.</note> positioned between heaven and earth, encircling the world in a perpetual circuit through the middle of the celestial sphere, carry out all the leadership of every age through their preceding movements. From that sphere, as if from a treasury of celestial power, they are seen to administer various heavenly decrees—collected within their circuit—to the subject world of inferior things below, by stretching forth the rays of their light. Therefore, since in the three books completed after the first one we have followed the nature and virtue of each of the 12 signs as well as the 7 stars, it follows that we should next pursue the fellowship of those stars with these signs in the effect of things, through a certain communion of nature and property. ¶ There are five principal <term>dignities</term><note>Dignities are conditions that increase a planet's power or "honor" in a specific location.</note> of the stars among the signs: **Domicile** (House), **Exaltation** <note>Latin: *principatus*. Usually refers to the planet's highest point of influence.</note>, **Bound**, **Trigon** (Triplicity), and **Decan** (Face); and **Joy**. There are two conditions contrary to these: **Fall** <note>Latin: *casus*.</note> and **Detriment** <note>Latin: *exitum*.</note>; and a third opposite to all, which is **Exile** <note>Latin: *exilium*. Often used interchangeably with detriment.</note>. After these are various degrees of good or evil effect between the two, which are not without some rational basis of opinion. From this, ancient investigation—with an indubitable knowledge of virtue—distributed them thus: for having followed the continuous returns of the stars through the signs over a long time with the greatest zeal, they saw that among the signs the nature of the stars flourished and their virtue increased, and conversely there [in other places] it did not. By observing the collection of signs and stars, they distributed dignity: sometimes for a similar distance in both, sometimes for the kindred nature and virtue of both, and sometimes for one or another fitness of consensus. And this is surely because, while all stars in every sign are seen in the effect of things among various mixtures of generations, they found much inequality and dissimilarity among those very mixtures through different places and the varying influence of each.

## ¶ On the Domiciles of the Stars. Chapter 2.

Beginning the distribution of dignities with the **Domiciles** <note>The "home" sign where a planet is most naturally itself.</note>, we first refute the less suitable opinion of certain people. Indeed, it is doubtful to no one that the domiciles of **Saturn** are Capricorn and Aquarius; of **Jupiter**, Sagittarius and Pisces; of **Mars**, Aries and Scorpio; of **Venus**, Taurus and Libra; of **Mercury**, Gemini and Virgo; of the **Sun**, Leo; and of the **Moon**, Cancer. Truly, however, there is no small dissonance [among authorities]. ¶ For there are those who, with foul speech...

<summary>This page outlines the various "dignities" or systems of planetary strength in astrology, listing the chapters to follow and defining the primary domiciles (houses) for the seven classical planets.</summary>
<keywords>astrology, planetary dignities, domiciles, decans, bounds, triplicities, Saturn, Jupiter, Mars, Venus, Mercury, Sun, Moon</keywords>

--- Page 64 ---
<meta>This page continues a technical discussion of astrological theory, specifically critiquing older models of planetary "bonds" or distances before introducing Ptolemy's system of planetary houses.</meta>

...misusing the quality of certain stellar circles and the properties of their natures, they assert that individual stars, in their daily courses, are never more or less distant from their path until they are bound to the Sun and Moon in a course of this kind. When the Sun held the 15th degree of Leo and the Moon held the 15th degree of Cancer, then each star occupied its own home <note>The "houses" or "domiciles" of the planets in the zodiac.</note> according to the length of its bond. For they say the bond of Mercury was 12 degrees and 30 minutes, which fell from the location of the Sun in order at the 7th degree of Virgo, and from the location of the Moon against the order at the 24th degree of Gemini. The bond of Venus, however, being 47 degrees and 11 minutes, fell from the Sun into Libra and from the Moon into Taurus. That of Mars was 78 degrees, from which it reached Aries and Scorpio. That of Jupiter was 120 degrees, extending as far as Sagittarius and Pisces. That of Saturn was 136 degrees, reaching as far as Capricorn and Aquarius. For this reason, they assert this partition of the domiciles.

We easily oppose these views as follows: If these degrees of the bonds are the degrees of the stars' direct motion, it is quite the opposite; for the degrees of direct motion for the superior stars <note>Mars, Jupiter, and Saturn.</note> are few, while those of the bonds are many. Likewise, if these degrees of the bonds are those by which, once passed by the Sun, it is necessary for the planets to become retrograde or direct, that might be possible. However, it is to the contrary, for although that path is curved, it does not retrograde by so many degrees. Mars indeed retrogrades much more. If these degrees represent how far the stars recede from the Sun, it would be 180 degrees for all the superior planets. If, however, their directness is no more than this, it is necessary for them to recede from the Sun. Therefore, these are not the cause for the partition of the domiciles. There are others who present various other equally foolish causes, which it would be idle to pursue further.

## ¶ Concerning the domiciles of the stars according to Ptolemy. Chapter 3.

<image-desc>Woodcut ornamental initial 'H' decorated with foliate scrolls and two circular blossoms, set within a double-lined square border.</image-desc>Having rejected those views, we must explain what credit Ptolemy <note>Claudius Ptolemy (c. 100–170 AD), the most influential astronomer/astrologer of antiquity.</note> gives to this partition. As he says, the leadership of the seven stars running through the twelve signs governs all such generation and corruption. Among these, a light more universal than the rest—in its power and effect throughout the whole world—is established. From these, the Moon, being near to the Earth and swiftest in its course, appears to have the greatest leadership in alteration among the stars. Since it is closest to us, its closest signs are also Cancer and Gemini. But Gemini is a masculine sign in which the Sun terminates the end of Spring; these two factors separated that sign from the nature of the Moon. Cancer, however, is a feminine sign, the beginning of Summer, and moist; thus the Moon, a feminine and moist star leading to the beginnings of things, claimed it.

¶ As for the Sun, just as it warms the world by its rising, the hottest time of the year is Summer. While it passes through Cancer and the following Virgo, it flourishes most in Leo, a masculine, hot, and dry sign. The Sun is also a masculine, hot, and dry star. Since Leo is the middle of Summer, just as the Sun is the middle of the stars <note>In the traditional geocentric order: Moon, Mercury, Venus, Sun, Mars, Jupiter, Saturn.</note>, the Sun occupied Leo. Furthermore, since the Sun is the measure of daylight and the Moon of the night, they move, govern, and sustain the generation of things through conjunction and opposition...

<summary>The author critiques an older theory of planetary houses based on specific degree-distances ("bonds") before explaining Ptolemy's system, which assigns Cancer to the Moon and Leo to the Sun based on their shared physical qualities.</summary>
<keywords>astrology, Ptolemy, planetary houses, zodiac signs, Leo, Cancer, planetary bonds, retrograde motion, celestial qualities</keywords>

--- Page 65 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the celestial houses and the planetary governance of the zodiac signs, following the logic of the luminaries (the Sun and Moon).</meta>

The influence of these planets is most manifest in these two signs: Cancer was granted to the Moon and Leo to the Sun. Since these two signs were granted to the luminaries for the leadership of generations of things and the sustenance of life during the summer, the opposite winter signs were rightly attributed to Saturn, which possesses a contrary effect. Thus, after the luminaries, the last of the planets <note>Saturn was considered the furthest planet in the ancient geocentric model</note> was assigned to the last signs, taking the count from the end; or the second-to-last planet, Jupiter, was assigned to the second-to-last signs. Sagittarius and Pisces were thus ordered, being suitable because they are in a friendly aspect <note>original: "figura"</note> to the homes of the luminaries. After these, Mars was placed, and not without reason, in an adverse aspect to the homes of the luminaries. Then Venus was placed in the remaining sextile <term>hexagono: an astrological aspect where signs are 60 degrees apart</term>, and since the others had received two signs each, the luminaries—as the parts of the world—divide the whole circle through the middle. For the Sun governs from the beginning of Leo to the end of Capricorn, and the Moon from the beginning of Aquarius to the end of Cancer, communicating with their lords in each of their respective halves.

### ¶ According to Hermes, after Abidemon. Chapter 4.

<image-desc>A large decorative drop cap initial 'D' containing a central six-petaled floral rosette surrounded by stylized foliage within a square frame.</image-desc>

Next is the place for the opinions of Hermes, who in his book introduces Abidemon <note>likely a reference to an ancient, possibly mythical, astrological authority</note> as the most ancient writer of astrology. He says that the twelve signs among the stars require a suitable partition. With diligent study, we find two different forms for each of the five planets, as they are sometimes oriental <note>rising before the sun</note> and sometimes occidental <note>setting after the sun</note>, sometimes retrograde and sometimes direct. However, the luminaries have only one form each. For the Sun never becomes oriental or occidental <note>in the planetary sense of phase</note>. Therefore, we understood that there would be two homes for each of the five planets to suit their dual natures, but for the luminaries, as they were of a simple form, there is only one. This is because some things are stronger in themselves than the other stars, just as everything that is simple in form and effect exists by the same nature, whereas that which is diverse is not firm in its composition. Furthermore, among the ancients, the Moon was called the "Star of the Sun," because while none of the others require foreign light, this one borrows as much as it shines from the Sun, with almost all its power derived from that same source. For nothing is fashioned from that source if there is no form, nor does form appear without matter; matter is the necessity of form, while form is the ornament of matter. Thus, since the Moon is, as it were, the matter of the Sun, and the Sun is the form of the Moon, it was the cause for the Moon to be rightly called the Star of the Sun, following the Sun with its own power. For this reason, the home of the Moon had to be contiguous <note>touching or adjacent</note> to the Sun's, and its rule and its days should immediately follow those of the Sun.

Having established these premises, we distribute the partition of the homes among the stars according to their natural fitness. For whatever is of a kindred nature is helped, but it is harmed by the contrary—just as water does not create fire but destroys it—yet it acts otherwise with things congruent to its nature. Therefore, since the heat of the entire world belongs to the Sun, and this heat is felt with full and complete power throughout the whole globe at the 15th degree of Leo, we began with Leo and the Sun, as they uniquely and entirely agreed in their nature: we gave Leo to the Sun. To the Moon, whose home was contiguous, we took the starting point from the 15th degree of Leo and moved in both directions by thirty degrees <note>original: "trigenis gradibus"</note>—the quantity of individual signs—reaching the 15th degree of Cancer on one side and the 15th degree of Virgo on the other. Thus, the Moon obtained one of these. Having distributed these, the star whose circuit...

<summary>This page explains the philosophical and astronomical rationale for assigning zodiac signs to specific planets, focusing on the relationship between the Sun (form) and the Moon (matter). It details the "Thema Mundi" or the birth chart of the world, starting the division of the zodiac from the 15th degree of Leo.</summary>
<keywords>astrology, zodiac houses, Hermes Trismegistus, Sun and Moon, planetary aspects, Leo, Cancer, planetary governance</keywords>

--- Page 66 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the planetary houses (domiciles) and their relationship to the luminaries (the Sun and Moon), following the traditional Ptolemaic system of celestial dignities.</meta>

The lunar law was the first to be embraced. From the houses of the Moon, two parts were drawn out on either side, as before, which designated Virgo and Gemini; these we have dedicated as the houses of Mercury <note>original: "mercurij domicilia". Mercury is assigned to Gemini and Virgo because they are adjacent to the houses of the Sun and Moon.</note>. After that, since the circle of Venus was the first from the houses of Mercury, two parts were likewise extended as before, and they assigned Libra and Taurus to Venus. Then, following the order of the circles, the extended parts assigned Aries and Scorpio to Mars, and the remaining signs to the remaining planets as they follow in sequence. The foundation of this partition is that the houses of Saturn—who brings the greatest misfortune of the world leading to the corruption of things—are opposite to the houses of the luminaries <note>the Sun and Moon</note>, which bring the highest fortune of the world leading to the generation of things. The houses of Mars, however, being somewhat less harsh, are in a square aspect <note><term>tetragona</term>: a 90-degree angle, considered a challenging or "tense" relationship in astrology</note>, which figure of opposition is somewhat lighter in misfortune. The houses of Jupiter, as a fortunate planet, are in a trine aspect <note><term>trigona</term>: a 120-degree angle, representing harmony and "greatest friendship"</note>, which was the figure of greatest friendship. Those of Venus are in a sextile aspect <note><term>exagona</term>: a 60-degree angle</note>, representing a lesser friendship, so that she too may descend from the fortune of Jupiter. Truly, since the heart of Mercury is nearly 19 degrees, and he is a lesser fortune than Venus, his houses are contiguous to the houses of the light <note>the Sun and Moon</note> by a quantity of nearly 12 parts.

¶ Some, however, distribute the houses of the stars by a certain contrariety: since the luminaries are bright, it is fitting that the houses of Saturn, who is dark, be opposite to them. Likewise, the Jovian <note>Jupiter's</note> houses are opposite to the Mercurial ones: of which one leads to the riches of the body, the other to the riches of the mind; we are not ignorant that these are indeed opposites. So too the Martian houses are opposite to those of Venus: of which the former looks toward war, anger, and horror, while the latter looks toward pleasures, jests, and gentleness. Thus also, although different planets render different causes, they all finally concur toward the same end. They add to this that the destruction of the stars is found in the opposites of the houses, and that the virtue of the Sun flourishes in all masculine signs, while that of the Moon flourishes in all feminine signs. Through this, the luminaries have a single form and a single house, while the others have two houses for their two figures. One house is suited for when they are oriental <note>rising before the sun</note> and direct in motion; the other for when they are occidental <note>setting after the sun</note> and retrograde. Saturn is suited to Capricorn for retrograding and for the occidental part, the immoderate cold of his nature increasing the nature of that sign. In Aquarius, he is suited to direct motion and the figure of oriental time, his nature tempering the nature of that sign. Thus Jupiter is in a better figure in Sagittarius and a worse one in Pisces; so Mars is more fit in Scorpio than in Aries. Venus, however, is adapted to Taurus for direct motion and the figure of the occidental part; and so Mercury in Virgo and the other in the rest. From this, two parts of the planetary houses proceed, taken from the luminaries and their houses, which bind the planets to the luminaries by a certain secret bond. This is done by collecting the number from the degree of the Sun up to 15 degrees of Leo, adding how much the Moon has traveled from her sign, and deducting the whole from the sign of the Moon by equal degrees. At the same hour, by collecting the number from the degree of the Moon up to 15 degrees of Cancer, adding how much the Sun has traveled from his sign, and deducting the whole from the sign of the Sun by equal degrees: if either of these parts falls into the house of any star, the other must necessarily fall into the other house; and if one falls into those which the luminaries occupy, the other will fall into the other.

### ¶ On the Lordship of the Stars according to other Astrologers. Chapter 5.

<summary>This page explains the geometric and symbolic logic behind assigning zodiac signs to planets, emphasizing the "aspects" (angles) like trines and squares that define their relationships. it also discusses how planetary strength changes based on whether a planet is moving "direct" or "retrograde."</summary>
<keywords>astrology, planetary houses, aspects, trine, square, sextile, Saturn, Jupiter, Mars, Venus, Mercury, Sun, Moon, zodiac signs, retrograde motion, celestial dignities</keywords>

--- Page 67 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of planetary dignities, specifically focusing on "exaltations" (here called princedoms or kingdoms). It transitions from a general critique of various astrological theories to the specific teachings of Ptolemy and Hermes.</meta>

Next, the order of our work demands that we follow with that dignity of the stars which we call **princedom** <note>original: "principatum"; commonly known in astrology as "exaltation"</note>, though others may call it a kingdom, a power, or by some other name. Indeed, there is no ambiguity among authorities—as we noted regarding the houses—that the princedom of the **Sun** is in the 19th degree of **Aries**; of the **Moon** in the 3rd of **Taurus**; of **Jupiter** in the 15th of **Cancer**; of **Mercury** in the 15th of **Virgo**; of **Saturn** in the 21st of **Libra**; of **Mars** in the 28th of **Capricorn**; of **Venus** in the 27th of **Pisces**; of the **North Node** <note>original: "capitis," the Head of the Dragon</note> in the 3rd of **Gemini**; and of the **South Node** <note>original: "caude," the Tail of the Dragon</note> in the 3rd of **Sagittarius**. The **fall** <note>original: "casus"</note> of each, however, is in the opposite degree of the opposite sign.

The causes for these assignments are debated among various authors, as to why they preferred these specific signs or these specific degrees over others. For **Ptolemy**, in dealing with the signs, attempts much but passes over the degrees; nor does he provide a suitable explanation for the signs themselves. **Hermes**, however—both the elder and the younger—following **Abidemon**, examines the matter with pure scrutiny. We shall therefore bring the teachings of both into the light, having first refuted the false opinions of certain others.

For there are many men of such impudent madness that they begin to hand down the teachings of a discipline before they even possess mastery of it. While they hold the attention of an audience with empty talk, they are forced by necessary error into whatever opinions console their sense of justice; thus they are led astray themselves and lead their foolish listeners with them. From this class of men, certain people professing astrology, having exhausted themselves in vain over the most subtle causes of things, finally fled from such labor to a "primordial cause"—a simple consolation for their ignorance.

They say, therefore, that those degrees are the princedoms of the stars because God, the creator of all things, first placed the stars and all the planets there at the first creation of the heavens. Since it was necessary for those bodies to occupy some initial locations, the Author chose those which were closest by nature and kinship. They claim that having progressed in their motion for many centuries afterward, the stars possess no more or less than the "mean" of those lights <note>the Sun and Moon</note> until, bound to the lights, they were allotted their houses according to the quantity of their bonds. Because we have dismissed this opinion more sparingly elsewhere, we can pass over it more quickly here.

¶ He asks, therefore: if God is the maker of all, to whom the infinity of all ages is but a moment, and He first willed the seven stars to be located in their princedoms—all having the same manner and motion—did He then, after some centuries had passed, as if repenting of His first will, prefer that they be altered from their first office? Does it follow that He was powerless over what He was to do next, or that He did not know at first what was most fit for nature? Furthermore, if the others are "bound" to the lights as you assert, why do they not move in retrograde in the same way that the lights bound to them move? For if they are bound to the lights because they vary in motion, why is it that the lights are not always carried with that same motion? What he asserts about them being first located in their princedoms is contrary to all the Indians, Persians, Chaldeans, and Greek astrologers, who all calculate the motion of the stars starting from the "Head of Aries" <note>the vernal equinox</note>, leading them through every diversity from the beginning until now.

---

### ¶ Concerning the princedom of the stars according to Ptolemy. Chapter 6.

Next, having lightly passed over the opinion of Ptolemy, we shall proceed to what follows. "Since we find," he says, "that the **Sun** ☉ ascends from **Aries** ♈ and increases the day; conversely, we understand its princedom to be there, and its fall in the other, **Libra** ♎. Indeed, since the nature of **Saturn** is contrary to the nature of the **Sun**, we perceive his princedom to be in **Libra** ♎, and his fall in the opposite."

<summary>This page discusses the "exaltations" (princedoms) of the planets, listing their specific zodiacal degrees and critiquing the theory that these positions were fixed by God at the moment of creation. The author favors the rigorous explanations of Hermes over the "lazy" theological justifications of his contemporaries.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of planetary dignities, specifically focusing on the "exaltations" or "principalities" (principatus) of the planets within the zodiac signs, following the system attributed to Hermes Trismegistus.</meta>

# Chapter 7.

The Moon <note>original: ☽</note> truly holds its principality <note>The "principality" or "exaltation" is a sign where a planet's influence is most powerfully felt.</note> in Taurus, because as the Sun <note>original: ☉</note> exists in Aries, the Moon begins to recede from the Sun and first begins to show its light in Taurus; its "fall" <note>The "fall" (casus) is the sign opposite the exaltation where the planet is weakest.</note> occurs in the opposite sign. Jupiter <note>original: ♃</note>, however, is in Cancer, to which belong the temperate winds that he mostly stirs up in Cancer. Mars <note>original: ♂</note> is in Capricorn, a southern sign, because it is opposite to Jupiter. Venus <note>original: ♀</note> is in Pisces, from which comes the moisture of spring; and Mercury <note>original: ☿</note> is in Virgo, from which the beginning of dryness comes. And he himself was temperately dry in his own nature; truly, the "falls" of each are in their opposite signs.

---

### ¶ According to Hermes, after Abidemon.

<image-desc>Decorative historiated initial 'N' showing a scene within an architectural frame.</image-desc>
**N**ow we shall introduce Hermes, as we are accustomed, changing not a single word of his speech. He says: All things which have a beginning do indeed, in their first origin, approach and grow; in their middle state they flourish; and in their end they decrease and recede. This is constant in every species of living being. Thus every star <note>In historical astronomy, "star" (stella) refers to both fixed stars and planets.</note> approaches and strengthens at the beginning of a sign, flourishes in the middle, and having diminished, recedes at the end; so it is when it is first oriental <note>Rising before the sun.</note>, and first moving directly, and so in the middle and the end. Therefore, the powers of the stars are generally in the middle of the signs. 

However, it is described elsewhere that Aries, Cancer, Libra, and Capricorn <note>original: ♈ ♋ ♎ ♑</note>—insofar as they are the quadrants of the circle and the beginnings of the seasons of the year—allot the principalities among the signs especially; hence we have begun the principalities of the superior stars from them. This is because Aries and Cancer stood in the part of approach, increase, and the height of the Sun. But Libra and Capricorn were in the part of recession and decrease. We have understood the principalities of the fortunate planets <note>The "benefics," Jupiter and Venus.</note> to be in Aries and Cancer, and those of the unfortunate ones <note>The "malefics," Saturn and Mars.</note> in Saturn <note>original: ♄</note> and Mars; for the principality of two is not in one sign, just as one sign is not the domicile of two. 

¶ Truly, because the Sun, beginning from Aries and ascending, brings forth the increase of the day, we have dedicated the principality of the Sun in that very sign, noting its most valid force in the middle, that is, at the 15th degree. But since all the power of the Moon is continuous with the Solar power, we have ordained the Moon's principality in Taurus, which followed Aries. 

But since I saw that darkness is contrary to light, and the Sun is light while Saturn is darkness, we began the principality of Saturn from the opposite of the Solar principality, as the first of the two <insert>malefics</insert>, with its greatest force noted in the middle. There remains Saturn and Mars as the second misfortune; and Cancer for Jupiter, who was previously the fortune closest to the lights <note>The Sun and Moon.</note>, with full power in the middle degrees. 

¶ Next are the principalities of the inferior planets: for since Venus recedes 47 degrees from the Sun, and the sign of Pisces was exceedingly moist, just as Venus is of a moister kind, she chose that sign rather than the triplicity <note>A group of three signs belonging to the same element.</note> of Saturn, with her greatest force in its 15th degree; likewise Venus <note>Note: The text likely means Mercury here, or is discussing the relationship between the signs</note> in Virgo at the 15th degree has her most solid power as if from the Taurus triplicity. For since <insert>Mercury</insert> recedes 27 degrees from the Sun, and Taurus was preoccupied by the Moon, Mercury obtained Virgo, which is of an autumnal nature related to himself. For just as he himself recedes significantly from the Sun, so Virgo is more closely related to Taurus; the cause of this is that the days of their rising through the right circle are equal. Then, where Saturn is placed opposite the Sun for a contrary leadership, so Mercury ought to be placed against Venus: for the former [Mercury] intends toward serious matters, while the latter [Venus] intends toward playfulness. 

¶ Having found the signs of the principalities in this manner, it is next convenient to determine the degrees themselves. We turn ourselves, therefore, to where we are accustomed, from which the series of all the partitions of heaven is most aptly ordained. That, however, is from the Sun, from midday, from the middle of heaven, from the equinoctial line, and from the beginning of Aries. For the Sun is the light and heat of the world; it is the day, in the middle of which the force of the Sun is whole; ascending from the head of Aries, the Sun brings forth increase. But since the rising of the signs varies for the whole world, in the middle of heaven through...

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the "Thema Mundi" or the birth chart of the world, specifically focusing on the precise degrees of planetary dignities and their mathematical relationships.</meta>

The whole world is the same as that which is balanced by light. Thus, since the degrees of the **Sun** ☉ <note>original: Sol</note> and the planetary **rulerships** <term>principatuum; the positions where planets exert their greatest influence</term> are the same for the entire world, we begin the fitting arrangement of these from the right circle <note>the celestial equator</note> and the middle of heaven <term>medium coeli; the highest point of the sky</term>. For we know that in the right circle, from the moment the first point of **Aries** ♈ first reaches the meridian line, the first point of **Cancer** ♋ emerges from the horizon. Whence mindful antiquity hands down that the rising of the nascent world emerged in **Cancer** ♋: of which no point is more fit for the beginning of a birth than that very degree in which **Jupiter** ♃ flourishes with full and integral virtue. Therefore, whenever in the right circle that degree—that is, the 15th of **Cancer** ♋—first emerges from the horizon, the 18th of **Aries** ♈ passes through the middle; we have established the rulership of the **Sun** ☉ in the 19th degree of **Aries** ♈, whose integral power is in the middle of the middle of heaven.

We know, moreover, that in heaven nothing is decreed without judgment and counsel. Therefore, when this judgment and counsel placed **Jupiter** ♃ in the rising of the world, it opposed him by diameter <note>an opposition aspect of 180 degrees</note> in **Aries** ♈, whose natures even unto today are repugnant to one another. For it is the office of a stellar rulership to be the surest sign of better leadership. When, therefore, we wished to determine the necessary certain degrees of the distances between them, we took the degrees of the distance of the stars from the **Sun** ☉, which distance is the cause of the diverse affections of the stars. We found this to be 12 degrees, within which every star is weaker, and we call those degrees the "distance."

Thus, when we added these degrees to the place of **Mars** ♂, the number was brought to the 27th of **Aries** ♈. We therefore designated the rulership of **Mars** ♂ in that degree. For we preferred to add rather than to subtract. For if that number were subtracted, it would place him in the 4th degree of that sign, in a place clearly remote and weak; and he would thus be applying to an opposition of **Jupiter** ♃, corrupting the application. For which reason, it was better for him to be separated from **Jupiter** ♃ and placed in a pivot <term>cardine; one of the four angular houses of a chart</term> fit for him.

Furthermore, since the domicile of the rulership of **Venus** ♀ was opposite to the Mercurial ones, and lest they be contaminated by an adverse figure between them, we added the degrees of distance, and the number was brought to the 28th of **Aries** ♈. But since misfortune, when approaching in fewer degrees than a fortunate star, causes harm, we placed **Venus** ♀ near **Mars** ♂: placing **Jupiter** ♃ closer so that they might bless him rather than be harmed by him, we established his rulership in the 27th degree of **Aries** ♈ in a place fit for his fortune. For if the degrees of distance had been subtracted, he would have fallen into a place contrary to the nature of **Mars** ♂.

**Mercury** ☿, however, fell into the sign and domicile of his rulership by right; he obtained the 15th degree of it, which was the strongest, fittingly bound to **Jupiter** ♃ by a friendly figure <note>likely a sextile or trine aspect</note> insofar as he was fortunate. For if [the distance] had been added, he would approach the opposition of **Venus** ♀. If subtracted, he would not be joined to **Jupiter** ♃ in the perfect virtue of the middle.

But truly, since the sign of the rulership of **Saturn** ♄ exists as a square <term>tetragonum; a 90-degree aspect</term> to the Jovial sign—and a square is half an opposition—we added half the distance, that is, 6 degrees: whence we terminated his rulership in the 21st degree of **Aquarius** ♒, a fit place in the pivot of the earth <note>the Imum Coeli or bottom of the sky</note>; which number, if it had been subtracted, would have cast him down from the pivot; and he would have been condemning **Jupiter** ♃, not **Jupiter** ♃ blessing him.

Truly, when the rulership of the **Moon** ☽ had fallen into **Cancer** ♋ and we wished to determine its degree, first regarding the diverse vision of the New Moon: since this sometimes appears within 12 degrees and sometimes in the 13th, under the equal line through the rising of the right circle that vision is detected; nevertheless, lengthening her rulership from the Solar one, we labored [to place it] in the 3rd degree of **Taurus** ♉, which vision is found to be of the same quantity with half the latitude...

in its southern latitude. For if we were to change the vision of the new moon <note>original: "nouiluniū visionē"</note> with southern latitude, its lordship would consist at the end of Gemini <note>symbol: ♊</note>. However, it was forbidden for two stars to hold lordship in their own sign; and the first leadership belonged to Mercury <note>symbol: ☿</note> of the Moon. Furthermore, the ascent of the Moon is from the Head of the Dragon <note>The North Node of the Moon</note>, just as the exaltation <note>original: "altitudo"</note> of the Sun <note>symbol: ☉</note> is in Gemini. For this reason, in Gemini—as the kinship of nature required—the lordship of the Head <note>North Node</note> fell. Seeking that very degree, we perceived this: that every star is in its best state when in the middle of its latitude, as far as that journey is concerned. Hence, since the Moon <note>symbol: ☽</note> in its middle latitude is distant from the Head by the quantity of one sign, we have established the lordship of the Head in the third degree of Gemini; and the Tail <note>South Node</note> in the opposite place, where all things are contrary to the Head. This, therefore, is the arrangement of the lordships of the stars, the foundation of which is that the lordships of the superior stars occupy the angles <note>cardinal points of the chart</note>, while the inferior stars also take places suitable for themselves, which are fortunate or unfortunate according as the King <note>likely referring to the Sun or the ruling principle of the chart</note> of generation is: first moved by the fortunate ones, then the corruption of the unfortunate effect follows. There are others who, as we use the distance of the stars from the Sun, employ the vision of the new moon. Similarly, they count 12 degrees because there the Moon renews the generations of things. They place Venus <note>symbol: ♀</note>, however, one degree less than Mercury <note>symbol: ☿</note> and Mars <note>symbol: ♂</note>, because she appears both before Mars and her body [is before] the body of Mercury.

->$\mathbb{C}$ On the methods and diversity of the terms of the stars. Chapter 8.<-

<note>The "Terms" or "Bounds" are specific subdivisions of each zodiac sign ruled by a particular planet.</note>

**H**aving established these matters, the diversity of the terms <note>original: "terminorum"</note> must be distinguished, which we find to be five-fold. For some are the terms of the Egyptians; others of the Chaldeans; others of Ptolemy; others of Aristotle; others of the Indians. If a certain rationale were held for these, they would not be uncertain themselves; except that they distributed the experiments of long-lived study. Among those things, however, that which is very often found to be correct by experiment rightly prevails. For this reason, among all the astrologers of almost all different nations, the terms of the Egyptians are found to be of more frequent use. These alone count the "greater years" of the stars in their entirety; they also receive misfortunes at the end of the signs and the most fortunate influences at the beginning. Therefore, having first noted the difference, we will then arrange each one. Aristotle, indeed, divides the terms of the signs among the seven stars <note>The five planets plus the Sun and Moon</note>, applying the reason that there ought to be no dignity among the signs for the stars since it is fitting for the Luminaries <note>The Sun and Moon</note> to be entirely exempt. All others, however, distribute those terms among only five stars <note>Mercury, Venus, Mars, Jupiter, Saturn</note>, because they receive the lights communicating through all the stars in their own domiciles. Some indeed assert that each Luminary communicates with its own lords in each sign in its own half of the circle. Others say the Sun has a part with the lords in all masculine signs, and the Moon in all feminine signs; both views are settled. For this reason, being content with these Luminaries, they dispensed with terms for them—a reason which seemed sufficient to the Egyptians. For certain others, accumulating various reasons, say that the Luminaries lack their own terms for this reason: because every diversity of nature has been weighed among the five stars. For from them comes the hot and dry, and the cold and dry (as Mars and Saturn); from the same, the hot and moist (as Jupiter); the cold (Venus); and the promiscuous <note>variable/neutral</note> (Mercury). Since, therefore, the Sun is of a Martian nature and the Moon of a Venusian nature, they remained content with their terms as if they were their own, with equal power through those terms for their own lords. Although...

<keywords>Lunar Nodes, Gemini, Terms and Bounds, Egyptian terms, Ptolemy, Aristotle, planetary natures, zodiacal dignities</keywords>

<meta>This page continues a discussion on the astrological "terms" (subdivisions of zodiac signs), comparing the systems of various ancient authorities including the Egyptians, Chaldeans, and Ptolemy.</meta>

Therefore, although their reasoning may differ, there is a consensus among all of them—except for Aristotle—that the lights <note>referring to the Sun and Moon</note> are excluded from the boundaries <note>The "terms" or *termini* are specific degrees within a zodiac sign ruled by the five planets: Saturn, Jupiter, Mars, Venus, and Mercury. The Sun and Moon do not rule terms.</note>. However, even if there is a general agreement on this matter, there is a specific discord, as none of them agree with the others regarding the order of the stars or the size of the terms. Furthermore, many of them assign the ends of the signs to the "unfortunate" stars <note>The malefic planets: Saturn and Mars</note>, while mostly beginning with the "fortunate" ones <note>The benefic planets: Jupiter and Venus</note>, because they understand the former to represent the end of things and the latter the beginning. Between these, the beginnings and ends of the signs fluctuate. Since the Egyptians, as has been said, preceded others in observing a greater number of stars through their own terms, they have rightly held authority over the rest. 

Although Ptolemy, when comparing the terms of the Egyptians and the Chaldeans, reports that he found what seemed to him the most correct system in a certain very ancient volume—which was partly destroyed and interrupted by a great expense of time, and partly obscured by the fog of many years—he was unable to discover the author's name despite long and wide searching. To avoid the disadvantage of an uncertain authority, he omitted those and published his own in imitation of them. Nevertheless, neither these nor any others, whether before or after Ptolemy, are found to have more frequent use or weightier authority than the terms of the Egyptians. For this reason, we will arrange them first, and then the others—not because we consider them necessary, but lest, being ignorant of them, we seem to prefer these, provided we have first excused the terms of the Chaldeans, with only the principles of the Indians being designated.

### On the Terms of the Egyptians. Chapter 9.

<image-desc>A decorative woodcut initial 'Q' featuring a central flower surrounded by scrolled foliage within a square border.</image-desc>

**Just as** Ptolemy and many other men of ancient authority, recalling the ancient histories of the world from memory, relate that after the universal flood—which, by covering the entire earth, deleted almost all memory of the prior age and left very few surviving souls—wisdom was first born among all the nations of the world in Chaldea <note>Ancient Mesopotamia</note> through the study of the celestial motion and power. Then, maturing with the passage of time, it was gradually derived into the rest of the world. Indeed, they relate that after the flood was over, as soon as the waters returned to their former channels and the dry land was revealed, Noah, surviving with his sons, followed the milder breezes from Armenia until he reached the place where Babylon later rose. Then, as the world was being reborn, his grandsons spread out from this center in every direction: along the Tigris as far as the Caspians, and from the Euphrates as far as the Kushites <note>original: "kusan"</note> as time progressed. 

Among these, they say that first one of his sons, Shem <note>original: "Sem"</note>—either instructed by the memory of life or enlightened by the divine gift of his own talent—began to marvel at the effects of following the celestial courses. From him, the study of the following age increased to such an extent that they grasped the entire heaven, both the circle of the zodiac <term>signifero circulo: the "sign-bearing" circle or Zodiac</term> and the stars running beneath it. First, they formed the power of each individual star, then, mixing them in a certain fellowship, they measured the effects of the stars through the revolutions of years, determining the "terms" just as they did the other dignities of the stars from the beginning of generation. From these people, as the human race grew, the other nations of the world later borrowed that wisdom and, applying study according to the capacity of their talent, corrected it somewhat and greatly increased it. Among these, the subtle ear of the Egyptians was of no small help. Therefore, the terms of the Chaldeans were established by that reasoning...

--- Page 72 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of astrological dignities and the division of the zodiac signs, following previous sections on planetary strengths.</meta>

arranged and deduced them through individual <term>trigones</term><note>Also known as triplicities; groups of three zodiac signs belonging to the same element (Fire, Earth, Air, Water).</note> in individual ways. When Ptolemy arranged these in his four-part work <note>original: "tetrastin", referring to the *Tetrabiblos*</note>—which the Arabs call the *Atarba*—he added that they were not, however, taken from any authentic source of his predecessors. For which reason, we also have deemed it necessary to omit them.

## ¶ Concerning the terms of the Indians. Chapter 10.

**T**he Indians, indeed, the first of the age—whether they held them thus from the beginning or they were recovered later through the invention of the Chaldeans <note>Ancient Babylonians</note> as it seemed more correct to them—ordered the <term>terms</term><note>Subdivisions of a zodiac sign where a specific planet has influence.</note> of the stars differently. They divide all masculine signs in one way and all feminine signs in another: beginning from Mars in the feminine and from Venus in the masculine; ending the masculine in Venus and the feminine in Mars. Thus, for Aries, the degrees are 5, 5, 4, 8, 5 for Jupiter, Venus, Mercury, Mars, and Saturn. <note>The text uses astrological symbols: ♃, ♀, ☿, ♂, ♄</note>. For Taurus, two degrees for Venus, then 5 for Mercury, 5, 8, 5, 5 for Jupiter and Mars. Just as Aries is divided, so are all masculine signs; as Taurus is, so all feminine signs are divided in the same order.

## ¶ Concerning the terms of the Chaldeans. Chapter 11.

**N**ext, we have arranged the lords of the trigones. Since the twelve signs represent four natures <note>The four elements: Fire, Earth, Air, Water</note> led through three intervals, it was necessary to have three signs of the same nature. Or, just as the zodiac marked three limits of the circle—namely Aries, Cancer, and Capricorn—so they are divided between them and within the trigone. Other reasons for this were explained in the second book. From this kinship of nature, it followed that something was added to the dignity of the stars. We say, therefore, that for masculine diurnal trigones, the masculine stars rule; for feminine and nocturnal ones, the feminine and nocturnal stars rule, which possess greater testimony and fitness in that recognition: <term>haizen</term> and <term>haubalict</term> <note>Latinized Arabic terms referring to a planet's "hayz" or dignity by being in a preferred hemisphere/sign</note> in the primary places. Therefore, of the lords of the Fiery Trigone, the first by day is the Sun; the second is Jupiter; by night it is reversed, and Saturn participates in both. Of the Earthy Trigone, the first by day is Venus; the second is the Moon; by night it is reversed, and Mars participates in both—and specifically in Virgo. Of the Airy Trigone, the first by day is Saturn; then Mercury; by night it is reversed, and Jupiter participates in both. Of the lords of the Watery Trigone, the first by day is Venus; the second is Mars; by night Mars precedes Venus, and the Moon participates in both.

## ¶ Concerning the decans and their lords according to the Persians, Babylonians, and Egyptians. Chapter 12.

**T**here follow the <term>decans</term><note>10-degree subdivisions of a sign, three per sign.</note>, which the Arabs in their language call "faces" <note>original: "facies"</note>. We shall first arrange them and their lords as the Persians, Chaldeans, and Egyptians use them; then we shall explain what the Indian opinion offers in its diversity. For all signs are divided into three affections, each part being ten degrees. Just as the stars share the signs themselves, which are distinguished by equal quantities through the order of the circles, so they ought to follow the equal parts of the signs in the same order. Therefore, beginning from Aries, the lord of the sign, Mars, occupies the first decan; the star of the following circle, the Sun, occupies the next; the third is Venus from the third circle. For the first decan of the second sign (Taurus), the next star of the fourth circle, Mercury, follows; the second is the Moon; the third is Saturn; and in this manner through the order.

It explains the planetary rulers assigned to these specific degrees and the elemental groupings of the signs.</summary>

### ¶ Concerning the decans and their lords according to the Indians. Chapter 13.

<image-desc>Decorative initial I featuring two personified faces or masks side-by-side within an ornate square frame.</image-desc>

**Now to the Indians** <note>original: "indis"; referring to the practitioners of Indian/Vedic astrology</note> it seemed otherwise; though they make the same partition, they do not follow the same order in the distribution. Indeed, they divide every sign into three parts: the second part is assigned to the lord of the 5th sign, and the third to the lord of the 9th sign, following the order of the signs of the triplicity <term>triplicity: a group of three zodiac signs belonging to the same element (Fire, Earth, Air, or Water)</term>. For example, in Aries, the first part belongs to Mars; the second to the Sun; the third to Jupiter; and so on in this manner. They believe they act rightly in this way so that the lords of the three signs within the triplicities rule over the three partitions of the signs in order. However, the former distribution <note>referring to the Egyptian/Chaldean system mentioned on the previous page</note>, being celebrated by more frequent use, has prevailed.

### ¶ Concerning the ninths of the signs. Chapter 14.

<image-desc>Decorative initial H featuring a child’s face surrounded by foliate patterns within an ornate square frame.</image-desc>

**These things being** ordered in this manner, next certain secondary powers of the stars throughout the circle must be explained. The first of these are the ninths, which those who receive them call the *navamsa* <note>original: "noubhariat"; a Latinization of the Arabic 'nubahriyat', derived from the Sanskrit 'navamsa' meaning 'ninth part'</note>. These practitioners, after the three-fold division of the signs, distribute them as if among the lords of the triplicity. They immediately cut each individual sign nine times, measuring each part at three degrees and twenty minutes <note>original: "triente"; one-third of a degree</note> out of the 20 points. They are seen to do these sections rightly because the same nature is found in the ninth sign from every sign. They distribute these among the stars in the order of the signs. For example, in Aries: the first ninth is Mars; the second Venus; the third Mercury; and so on in order until the ninth part, which the lord of the ninth sign, Jupiter, obtains. In Taurus, however, the first is Saturn; the second Jupiter; the third Mars; the fourth the Sun; and so on in this manner. The discovery of the lord of such a part is easy: for from every triplicity of a tropical sign <term>tropical sign: the signs marking the start of seasons (Aries, Cancer, Libra, Capricorn)</term>, the first ninths are allotted through the individuals of that triplicity; the second ninths to the lord of the following sign, and so on in order. For example, for the Fire triplicity, Mars; for Earth, Saturn; for Air, Venus; for Water, the Moon. Some, however, distribute these ninths through the orders of the circles, beginning each sign from its own lord. For example, in Aries: the first is Mars, the second the Sun. In Taurus: the first is Venus, the second Mercury, the third the Moon, the fourth Saturn, and so on in this manner; however, the former method has prevailed.

### ¶ Concerning the twelfths of the signs. Chapter 15.

<image-desc>Decorative initial P featuring ornate floral and foliate scrollwork within a square frame.</image-desc>

**After this**, the twelfths <term>twelfths: also known as 'dodecatemoria', these are 2.5-degree subdivisions of a zodiac sign</term> of the signs must be treated, and the lords of each degree must be found. For just as the circle itself was divided into 12 spaces, so it was fitting that each of those spaces be subdivided into the same number of equal parts, it being understood that each individual sign has a kinship with all the others. Therefore, they measure each part as two and a half degrees—that is, 150 minutes. The first part of each belongs to the sign itself, and the following parts to the following signs in order. The discovery of this system is easy: for the total distance from the beginning of the sign to the specific degree whose twelfth we seek is multiplied by twelve. Then, the whole sum is counted out from the beginning of that sign through each sign by thirty degrees; wherever it stops, the twelfth of that degree is found in that sign. ¶ Hermes <note>Hermes Trismegistus, the legendary founder of astrology</note> and his followers, however, were the first to distribute the natures of the individual degrees so that the first degree of any sign drew the nature of the sign itself; the second, the nature of the second sign; the third, of the third, and so on in order. For example, the twelve...

<keywords>astrology, decans, Indian astrology, navamsa, dodecatemoria, triplicities, planetary lords, Hermes Trismegistus, zodiac subdivisions</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of astrological subdivisions, specifically the "duodenaria" (twelfth-parts) and the qualitative classification of individual degrees within the zodiac signs.</meta>

...ending in the 12th part, the 13th should then begin from the same point. By this artifice, Hermes <note>Refers to Hermes Trismegistus, the legendary founder of Hermeticism and a primary authority in ancient astrology</note> in his books concerning various matters of both nativities <note>birth charts</note> and questions, handed down many techniques, assigning the lords of related signs to each individual degree. Nor indeed should it be thought that the twelfth-parts of the stars are the same as those of the signs. For whatever sign any star may be in, once its degrees have been taken twelve times and the total sum calculated from the beginning of the sign, it designates the twelfth-part of the stars.

### ¶ Concerning Masculine and Feminine Degrees. Chapter 16.

<image-desc>Woodcut decorative initial 'U' containing the portrait of a scholar in a circular frame, wearing a cap and robes.</image-desc>

Since this is the case, it follows that among these degrees some are masculine and others feminine. We shall first set forth the opinions of various authors, and then we will arrange those which are in more frequent use; yet we shall ensure that all are in agreement. For the utility of this kind of distinction is surely this: that in either a birth chart of males or a question proposed concerning males, if masculine stars coincide in masculine signs and degrees, they strengthen the leadership <note>or "guidance" of the planet</note>, and likewise in the other case <note>for females</note>. Furthermore, some others posit that for masculine signs, the first 12 and a half degrees are masculine, and the same number remaining are feminine. Regarding feminine signs, they do the opposite: the first 12 and a half are feminine, and the same number following are masculine. Others, however, discern the sex by twelfths, judging the first parts of masculine signs to be masculine, and the first of feminine signs to be feminine, alternating then in order until the end. Now we shall arrange those that remain, provided we have first explained another distinction of degrees, so that we may arrange those following these in a continuous sequence.

### ¶ Concerning Bright and Dark Degrees. Chapter 17.

<image-desc>Woodcut decorative initial 'I' depicting the biblical scene of the Sacrifice of Isaac: Abraham holds a sword over Isaac on an altar, while an angel appears from a cloud and a ram is seen in the thicket.</image-desc>

Now, for the same reason, among the same degrees of the circle, some judge certain ones to be bright, others dark, others indeed middling or "shadowy" by a certain condition, and others empty. Their utility is that the bright degrees certainly add to the dignity of matters, the dark ones do the opposite, and the middling ones are between the two.

### ¶ Concerning the Wells of the Stars. Chapter 18.

<image-desc>Woodcut decorative initial 'P' featuring elaborate floral scrollwork and a small human face integrated into the design.</image-desc>

Finally, there are various degrees throughout the different signs that act as either aids or obstacles to the stars. Those which stand in the way are called <term>wells of the stars</term> <note>original: "putei stellarum"</note> in the Arabic language, which we are accustomed to call "pits" or "precipices" <note>original: "præcipitia"</note>. Those which are favorable, adding to one's luck, have taken their name from the matter itself <note>i.e., "fortunate degrees"</note>. Pits are of such a function that whatever star they receive, they diminish its power. Therefore, they are always dangerous for fortunate stars, and never healthy for misfortunes, provided that they diminish their harmful power. Since the truth of these is uncertain among many, we—having omitted the various opinions which the Egyptians themselves hand down—shall arrange them, provided that once the reasoning for those adding to fortune is explained, we may dispose of both continuously. For antiquity has found by experience the reasoning for the degrees that add to fortune: that when stars have led toward fortune according to the favorability of their locations, if either the Moon <note>original symbol: ☽</note> or the <term>Part of Fortune</term> <note>A calculated point in a chart representing prosperity</note> possesses these degrees, or if any of them should be the degree of the Ascendant <note>original: "orientis"</note>, they add fortune to the native. Among which there are some such that if the leading stars also...

...have inclined toward the circumstances of the newborn. These degrees, applied in this manner, are later recovered in the subdivision <note>The author refers to the practice of dividing zodiac signs into smaller portions for more precise predictions</note>. There are, therefore, three from Taurus: 15, 28, 30; two from Leo: 3, 5; from Scorpio: 4, 7; from Aquarius: 4, 11. Besides these, there are others distinguished through each individual sign in which ancient experience nonetheless agrees: that whenever these degrees themselves are rising, or during the day the Sun <note>original: ☉</note> and at night the Moon <note>original: ☽</note> occupy a suitable place in the circle, they promise to the newborn a sublime level of honors, such as kingdoms or principalities closest to kingdoms. These are the dignities of the stars throughout the whole circle—unfavorable aids and obstacles—besides which, something is added to each from the mixture of both types, which we shall follow in their proper places as they occur in detail. Yet let no one think us ignorant that, besides these, the Indians use various other partitions of the circle and dignities of the stars, suited indeed to their own skill; but since these are neither suitable nor necessary for our method of judging, to follow them further would suggest the beginning of a loss to this refined work.

# On the Nature of the Stars. Chapter 1.

<note>original: "De natura stellarum. Cap. 1."</note>

At the beginning of this treatise, we followed the natures of the stars and the signs partly individually and partly through their shared influences. Next, we shall follow the universal leadership of the stars, first in this sign and then in the following ones. First, it is necessary that we explain the reasoning behind the forms <note>These "forms" refer to the constellations and the specific images associated with degrees of the zodiac</note> that arise through the leadership of each sign. Indeed, these concepts, being so far removed from our physical senses, are scarcely grasped by the intellect; they drive men into such weighty opinions and great wonder. 

Even the greatest part of the **astrologers**, when they read of the rising of these forms but did not reach an understanding of their "leadership" <note>The specific influence or governing power of a star</note>, were led to the opinion that the matter was either entirely ineffective or perhaps an effect beyond human understanding. However, the Persian astrologers **Hermes** and **Alcalius**, as well as the greatest of the Indians, have left behind in their own books on elections <note>Electional astrology: choosing the best time to begin an undertaking</note> the qualities and powers of these forms, sought out with skillful investigation. They committed the leadership of these forms to the reader's talent and intellect, partly through descriptions of their figures, partly through their properties, and partly through an exposition of their qualities. 

However, because the specific parts were so deeply remote, they neither knew when they occurred nor did they pass down that knowledge, not having experienced the celestial power through a specific artifice. Since the leadership of these parts is not the focus of this current business, we shall defer them to another time. 

Yet, it must not be thought that those who attained knowledge of this matter and left it to posterity intended for us to understand that such things actually exist in the heavens as physical bodies, or are shaped in such a way, or are imbued with these qualities in their essence as these forms rise above the face of the earth through the individual degrees and **deans** <note>term: decanos (decans). These are 10-degree subdivisions of a zodiac sign, each ruled by a different planet.</note> of the signs. 

Rather, they saw that various events in the world follow specific locations in the circle and individual decans of the signs. Indeed, some even went so far as to deny that the signs and degrees of the circle had any leadership over affairs at all, asserting instead that forms are carried from above which, by drawing out figures related to the things of the lower world, drive them into various outcomes. 

When they saw this, I say, they spoke of forms agreeable to such influences throughout the signs, so that they might direct the intellect of those distracted by more familiar speech. They used shadowed speech to both hide the secrets of heaven and to designate them with full understanding to a wise mind. Thus, they distinguished them among themselves with various names and descriptions—some related to our own human kind, others unheard of and foreign to our imagination. They employed this style specifically to indicate that a great distinction must be made between the forms of the lower world and the superior world. 

We find, therefore, among all the writers of this art, a threefold diversity of celestial forms. We have already spoken of some of these, and others remain to be discussed—notwithstanding that some writers attribute other things and forms to the heavens. Since those are not part of this present work, we leave them to their own treatises. 

Regarding those we do accept, we shall first explain those forms and their locations in which the **Chaldeans** and **Egyptians** agree; then we shall apply the findings of the Indians. Finally, we shall arrange the rising of the 48 images <note>The 48 classical constellations</note> which **Allatus** and **Ptolemy** describe. Since these are individually composed of their own stars, which must necessarily change their locations in the circle over time due to their motion, according to Ptolemy...

<summary>This page introduces a treatise on the nature of stars and constellations, arguing that zodiacal "forms" are symbolic tools used by ancient masters like Hermes and Ptolemy to explain celestial influences on Earth.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the astronomical and astrological discourse, specifically focusing on the rising of the zodiac signs and their decans (ten-degree subdivisions) as established by ancient authorities.</meta>

<sig>e 3</sig>

A great comparison having been made regarding their progress. We measure the rising of these stars as the Albumasar <note>Abu Ma'shar (787–886 AD), a highly influential Persian astrologer whose works were translated into Latin and shaped European science.</note> of our time does: namely, setting the limit at the year 1160 of Alexander <note>The Seleucid era, commonly used in medieval astronomical tables.</note>, and he left the method of proceeding to us. Others, however, assign each of the Indians <note>Refers to the Indian astrological tradition, which was often contrasted with Greek or Persian methods in medieval texts.</note> to themselves, rising from those same unmoved places always in the same settings. For these are not stars as those others are; rather, they are certain markers of the higher circle, and the forms of the indicators rise in each of the decans <note><term>Decan</term>: A subdivision of a zodiac sign consisting of ten degrees. Each sign has three decans, each with its own symbolic ruler and imagery.</note>, some in each and others in many. This is our privilege: that just as Albumasar varies nothing concerning these forms from their first discovery, so our translation shall neither change one of his words, nor add or subtract a single point.

### On the Natures of the Signs. Chapter 2.

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration of Aries the Ram, the first zodiac sign, standing on a patch of earth with sparse grass, facing right with curled horns.</image-desc>

Aries is by nature fiery, bitter in taste, of upright stature, of two colors, and of two forms; it increases the day beyond 12 hours, and its rising is less than 30 degrees. In its **first decan**, as the Persians <note>original: "Perse"</note> relate, there rises a woman whose name is Daughter of Splendor; after her, the tail of the Sea Fish, the beginning of the Eridanus <note>The celestial river constellation.</note>, and the head of the Stag-Bull—that is, a form produced from a stag and a bull. After these, a dog-headed man <note>original: "cynocephalus"</note> carrying a candle in his left hand and a club in his right. According to the Indians, a black man with red eyes, large of body, brave and spirited, standing on the ridges of the forests, clothed in a white linen cloth—one of the 48 images. According to the Greeks and Ptolemy, the back of Cepheus (whom the Arabs call the Lord of the Sun) and his buttocks and knees, as well as the left hand and the middle of the back of Andromeda, and the buttocks and left thigh of the second Fish, and also the flaxen thread—that is, the intestine of the Whale <note>original: "ceti"; refers to the constellation Cetus.</note>.

In the **second decan** of Aries, according to the Persians: the middle of the Sea [Fish], the middle of Eridanus, the middle of the Stag-Bull, a ship, a horse carrying the sky in its hand, a woman combing her head with iron arms, the head of Medusa, and the curved sickle of Perseus (which the Arabs call the Cloud of Perseus); a woman dressed in fine linen and red cloths, with one foot like a horse, thinking of going to seek cloths, necklaces, and pearls. According to the Greeks and the 48 star-bearers: the thigh of Cepheus the Lord of the Sun, the shins with the feet, the head of the Fish, the left hand and right feet of Andromeda, the head of Eridanus, the head and horns of the Ram, and the rest of the thread with the breast of the Whale.

In the **third decan** of Aries, according to the Persians: a youth named Sallus sitting on a throne, with whom is a double horse and the hind parts only of one born failing [or crying out] to the gods; also the breast of the Fish and the hind head of Eridanus, the tail of the Stag-Bull, and the second half of the forehead. According to the Indians: a man of yellow color with red hair, holding wooden tools in his hand and a rod for the flock, dressed in red, skilled in the craft of iron and eager to do it; for this is the decan of Jupiter, as it pleases the ancients, yet it does not prevail because it is the house of Mars. According to the Greeks, from the stars: the breast of the Fish with the left hand by which he carries the head of Medusa, and a long star in the head of the Ram; also the belly of the Ram and the head of the Whale.

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration of Taurus the Bull, the second zodiac sign, depicted reclining or leaping toward the left, positioned next to a wooden post or tree stump.</image-desc>

Taurus is by nature earthy, sour in taste, increasing the day but diminished in nature. In its **first decan**, as the Persians teach, Orion rises girt with a sword, holding a sword in his left hand and a spear in his right; above his hand are two candelabra addressing him and calling him by the name Orion...
